User: how do i return a value from a lambda without a return keyword
Joe: Just write the expression; the body value is returned automatically, as in `lambda x: x * 2`.

User: why would you force a parameter to be positional-only
Joe: It stops a caller from binding it by name, which is useful when the name is common and collisions would be confusing.

User: why can a generator not report its length
Joe: `len` is not part of the iterator protocol, so calling it on a generator raises `TypeError`.

User: how do i find the first non-whitespace character in a string
Joe: `next(c for c in s if not c.isspace())` walks forward and stops at the first real character.

User: why does slicing past the end not raise
Joe: Slices clamp silently to the sequence bounds, unlike integer indexing which raises `IndexError`.

User: whats the point of `operator.itemgetter` over a lambda for indexing
Joe: It is a C-implemented callable, so it avoids the interpreter overhead of a Python lambda in hot sorting code.

User: how do i make a function accept any single argument or none
Joe: Use a sentinel default like `_missing = object()` and compare identity against it.

User: is a list comprehension faster than a plain for loop with append
Joe: Yes, the comprehension builds the list in one C-level pass and skips the repeated attribute lookup on `append`.

User: whats the point of `lstrip(chars)` over `lstrip`
Joe: Passing a set of characters strips any of them, so `s.lstrip("xy")` removes runs of x and y from the left.

User: what causes `IndexError` where a slice would have worked
Joe: Direct indexing checks bounds strictly, while a slice clamps, so only direct access can overflow.

User: is `itemgetter(0, 1)` valid for pulling two keys at once
Joe: Yes, it returns a tuple of those elements, which is handy as a sort key without a lambda frame.

User: what keeps `*args` from being confused with `**kwargs`
Joe: `*args` collects extra positional values into a tuple, while `**kwargs` collects extra keywords into a dict.

User: why do i not see the loop variable leak after a comprehension in python 3
Joe: A comprehension has its own scope, so the variable it binds disappears when the comprehension finishes.

User: what stops me from stripping meaningful leading characters
Joe: Use a plain `.strip()` only for whitespace, and pass an explicit character set when you only want to strip specific characters.

User: how do i delete a run of items in one statement
Joe: `del xs[i:j]` removes the whole slice, and it costs one shift instead of one per item.

User: how do i make `attrgetter` work for a nested attribute path
Joe: Chain them, as in `attrgetter("a.b")` in 3.11 or by nesting `attrgetter` calls for older versions.

User: how do i accept keyword arguments of unknown names in a typed function
Joe: Add `**kwargs` to the signature, since `**` is the only way to receive arbitrary keywords.

User: what happens if a comprehension references a variable assigned inside itself
Joe: The name is local to the comprehension, so referencing the same name before the assignment runs raises `UnboundLocalError`.

User: why is building a string with a list comprehension and join idiomatic
Joe: It runs the work in C and allocates once, whereas appending in a loop repeatedly reallocates the growing string.

User: whats the point of assigning to a slice
Joe: `xs[i:j] = other` replaces a run in place, which is how you splice lists without rebuilding them.

User: why is a lambda less readable than a def for anything multi-line
Joe: It cannot span lines or hold statements, so complex logic has to be crammed into one expression.

User: what makes a keyword-only argument with no default required
Joe: It has no default, so omitting it raises `TypeError` even if later parameters have defaults.

User: why does a generator expression use parentheses while a list comp uses brackets
Joe: The parentheses match the older generator function syntax and force lazy evaluation, while brackets force eager building.

User: how do i avoid the quadratic cost of `s += piece` in a hot loop
Joe: Accumulate into a list and `''.join` at the end, or use `io.StringIO` and call `.getvalue()` once.

User: what happens if the right side of a slice assignment has a different length
Joe: The list is resized to match, so the slice assignment acts as both an insert and a delete.

User: when should a lambda stay a lambda
Joe: Keep it inline as a sort key or callback of a few characters; anything longer deserves a named function.

User: how do i give a keyword-only parameter a default in the middle
Joe: Put `*, name=default` there; the order among keyword-only parameters does not affect defaults.

User: whats wrong with wrapping a generator expression in a list
Joe: It builds every item in memory, which defeats the laziness that made the generator worth using.

User: what makes `io.StringIO` a good buffer for many small writes
Joe: It appends into an internal buffer instead of rebuilding the string each time, so total cost stays linear.

User: how do i replace the contents of a list without changing its identity
Joe: Assign to a slice, like `xs[:] = new_list`, so every existing reference now sees the new contents.

User: what makes a def statement slower to call than a lambda body
Joe: A def creates a function object and frame per call exactly like a lambda; the cost difference is in object creation, not calling.

User: why does a positional-only parameter block a key argument
Joe: `/` removes the keyword option entirely, so `f(x=1)` raises even though the name matches.

User: what keeps a comprehension from being slower than an explicit loop
Joe: Nothing much; the comprehension is usually equal or faster because the append lookup and loop setup happen in C.

User: how do i tell whether a string contains any letter at all
Joe: `any(c.isalpha() for c in s)`, which short-circuits on the first letter instead of scanning the whole string.

User: why would anyone assign to a slice at all
Joe: It avoids rebinding the name, so other holders of the list object observe the change instead of keeping the old one.

User: is a def created inside a loop reused across iterations
Joe: No, each iteration creates a fresh function object, so late binding on captured variables still bites.

User: how do i tell whether a signature is already positional-only
Joe: `inspect.signature(f)` shows the `/` marker, or check each parameter's `kind` against `POSITIONAL_ONLY`.

User: how do i tell whether something is a list or a tuple at runtime
Joe: Check `isinstance(x, (list, tuple))`, or compare `type(x) is list` for an exact match that rejects subclasses.

User: why does `str.replace` do nothing when the count argument is 0
Joe: The count limits replacements to zero, so the original string is returned untouched.

User: what makes `xs[:] = xs` a way to drop duplicates by accident
Joe: The whole list is replaced by itself, and any dedup inside the comprehension changes the length and therefore the contents.

User: how do i keep a loop body from rebinding a shared helper each time
Joe: Define the helper outside the loop, or use `functools.partial` so one object serves every iteration.

User: what makes a lambda with a default argument easy to misread
Joe: The default sits inside the parameter list, so `lambda x, y=1: ...` reads like two arguments rather than one plus a default.

User: why is a tuple hashable and a list not
Joe: Tuples are immutable, so their hash cannot change after creation, which is what hashing requires.

User: what happens if you pass `count` larger than the real number of hits
Joe: Every occurrence up to that limit is replaced, so an over-large count behaves the same as no limit.

User: how do i compare two lists ignoring element order
Joe: Sort copies first or use `set(a) == set(b)`, since list equality is order-sensitive.

User: how do i keep a long lambda from becoming unreadable
Joe: Move it to a named `def`, since a lambda cannot be formatted across lines or documented.

User: what happens when you try to change a tuple's element
Joe: Tuples have no `__setitem__`, so assignment raises `TypeError: 'tuple' object does not support item assignment`.

User: how do i toggle a multi-character replacement in one call
Joe: Use `str.replace` on a placeholder twice, or build a `dict` for `re.sub` so each token maps to its own replacement.

User: what stops me from sorting a list of dicts directly
Joe: Dicts are unordered inside, so compare with an explicit `key=` that extracts the field you care about.

User: can i concatenate a tuple and a list
Joe: No, `tuple + list` raises `TypeError` because the sequence types must match; convert with `list(t)` first.

User: whats wrong with assuming `split` returns a fixed-length result
Joe: The length depends entirely on the data, so any code indexing a fixed position must handle the short case.

User: how do i sort a list of dicts by one field
Joe: `sorted(rows, key=lambda r: r["name"])` orders by that field while leaving the dicts intact.

User: why does `s * 3` work for strings but not for lists plus tuples
Joe: Both are sequences and define `__mul__` as repetition, but `s * 3` repeats the same string while `+` would concatenate two different ones.

User: why does a trailing separator in csv-style text produce an empty field
Joe: The separator is still data, so the final field is present and empty rather than absent.

User: what makes sorting mixed-case text give a surprising order
Joe: Plain string compare is codepoint order, so all uppercase letters sort before all lowercase; use `str.lower` as the key.

User: what keeps `int` from overflowing the way `float` does
Joe: Python integers are arbitrary precision, so `2 ** 1000` is an exact value rather than an overflow error.

User: how do i drop empty fields after a split
Joe: `[p for p in s.split(",") if p]` filters falsy pieces, which removes both empty strings and stray whitespace.

User: how do i get a natural sort where item2 comes before item10
Joe: Split out the digits and pass them as a key, like `key=lambda s: [int(t) if t.isdigit() else t for t in re.split(r"(\d+)", s)]`.

User: how do i check the memory a value occupies
Joe: `sys.getsizeof(x)` reports the object size in bytes, though containers exclude the memory held by their elements.

User: what makes `str.splitlines` slower than a manual `find` loop on huge input
Joe: It builds every line into memory at once, while a find loop with an index lets you process one at a time.

User: what causes `zip(xs, ys)` to be the wrong tool for a dict build
Joe: It pairs by position, so duplicate keys would silently collapse; build the dict in a loop with explicit assignment instead.

User: why does `sys.getsizeof` understate a list's real footprint
Joe: It only measures the list object itself, so separately sum the sizes of every element to get the true total.

User: how do i translate between character and its case simultaneously
Joe: `str.maketrans("abc", "ABC")` returns a table you pass to `translate`, doing it in one pass.

User: how do i build a dict from two lists with a clear failure on mismatch
Joe: Loop with `zip(strict=True)` or check `len` first, so a length error surfaces instead of a truncated result.

User: what happens if you add a str and an int with `+`
Joe: `TypeError: unsupported operand type(s) for +`, because `+` is overloaded for like types and does not coerce.

User: why does `str.translate` outrun a loop of `replace` calls
Joe: It walks the string once with a lookup table instead of rescanning the whole string for each substitution.

User: why is `dict.fromkeys(xs)` a trap for mutable values
Joe: Every key maps to the same one object, so mutating one list value mutates them all.

User: how do i make `int` and `str` combine in one expression
Joe: Convert explicitly with `str(n)` or `int(s)`, since `+` will not guess which side you meant.

User: what makes `str.center` the wrong tool for aligning table output
Joe: It centers rather than anchoring left, so columns drift when widths differ; `ljust` with an explicit width is predictable.

User: how do i build a dict of new empty lists per key
Joe: `{k: [] for k in keys}`, because the comprehension builds a fresh list per iteration.

User: why can you multiply a list by a tuple
Joe: You cannot; `list * tuple` raises `TypeError` because repetition needs the same type on both sides.

User: how do i format a table column that must line up on the right
Joe: `f"{value:>{width}}"` pads on the left to the chosen width, which keeps the digits flush right.

User: what makes `min` on an empty sequence raise
Joe: There is no smallest element to return, so it raises `ValueError: min() arg is an empty sequence`.

User: whats the point of `bool` being falsy in an `if` chain
Joe: It lets you write plain values without `!= None` guards, so an empty container naturally means nothing to do.

User: whats the point of a fixed field width in the format spec
Joe: It makes generated output deterministic and diff-friendly, since the column positions stop depending on the value length.

User: how do i get a default out of `min` on a possibly empty list
Joe: Pass `default=`, as in `min(xs, key=..., default=None)`, which returns the default instead of raising.

User: when should i reach for `isinstance` over an exact `type` check
Joe: Use `isinstance` whenever a subclass should pass, which is almost always what you want for duck-typed libraries.

User: what makes a bytes format like `b"%d" % n` behave differently
Joe: The `%` operator on bytes has no int rule of its own, so `b"%d" % 5` raises `TypeError`.

User: what does the `key` argument to `max` actually receive
Joe: Each element, one at a time; the function computes the comparison value and the original element is what comes back.

User: how do i tell an empty string from a string of spaces
Joe: `bool(s.strip())` collapses both cases, or compare against `""` after stripping if whitespace is not meaningful.

User: how do i tell whether two strings differ only by case and whitespace
Joe: Compare `a.strip().casefold()` against `b.strip().casefold()` to normalize both sides before the check.

User: how do i find the item with the highest score, not the highest score
Joe: `max(items, key=lambda i: i.score)`, since returning the key value would lose the item itself.

User: what causes a `bytes` object and a `str` to never compare equal
Joe: They are different types and have no conversion rule, so `b"a" == "a"` is `False` rather than a comparison.

User: what causes a UnicodeError when printing to a narrow console
Joe: The console encoding cannot represent a character in your string, so reconfigure with `sys.stdout.reconfigure(errors="replace")`.

User: why is `sum` with a generator slower than with a list
Joe: A list is a tight C iterable while a generator adds a Python-level frame per item, so the sum spends more time dispatching.

User: why can a set contain `None` alongside other values
Joe: `None` is hashable and ordinary, so it takes its own slot like any other member.

User: how do i keep printing safe on a terminal with a limited codepage
Joe: Use `errors="backslashreplace"` so unencodable characters print as escapes instead of raising.

User: what keeps `abs` working on custom numeric types
Joe: It dispatches to the type's `__abs__`, so any class defining that method gets correct behaviour for free.

User: why does splitting on `isdecimal` differ from `isdigit`
Joe: `isdigit` accepts superscripts like `²`, while `isdecimal` only accepts characters valid as base-10 digits.

User: how do i round only the last two digits of a number in place
Joe: Use a format spec like `f"{x:.2f}"` for display, or `round(x, 2)` for a real numeric result.

User: how do i validate that a string is a plain ASCII identifier
Joe: `s.isidentifier()` together with `s.isascii()` covers both the character rules and the ASCII restriction.

User: what makes `round(x, 2)` still a float
Joe: The ndigits argument changes only the scale, not the type, so the result stays a float.

User: what keeps a keyword from being used as a variable name
Joe: `str.iskeyword(s)` reports reserved words, so you can pick an alternative before the `SyntaxError` appears.

User: why can `round(x, 2)` still print long in a repr
Joe: The stored float is the nearest binary value to the rounded decimal, and `repr` shows the shortest exact form.

User: how do i check whether a name would shadow a builtin
Joe: Test `name in dir(builtins)`, since shadowing `list` makes the original unreachable inside that scope.

User: how do i get a decimal that prints exactly as typed
Joe: Use `decimal.Decimal("1.10")`, which keeps the scale you constructed instead of collapsing to a float.

User: what causes a `Decimal` division to need a precision argument
Joe: Division can be non-terminating, so the context precision decides where the result is rounded.

User: how do i compare floats using a tolerance that scales with magnitude
Joe: `math.isclose(a, b, rel_tol=1e-9)` compares against a relative difference, which suits growing values.

User: whats the point of `abs_tol` versus `rel_tol`
Joe: `abs_tol` is a fixed floor useful near zero, where a relative test alone would be too strict.

User: why does a `Fraction` avoid the float precision problem
Joe: It stores numerator and denominator as exact integers, so arithmetic never rounds.

User: how do i turn a float into an exact `Fraction`
Joe: `Fraction(x).limit_denominator()` finds the closest small-denominator rational instead of the exact binary value.

User: what makes `Fraction(0.1)` ugly
Joe: The float 0.1 is not exactly one tenth, so the exact fraction has a huge denominator; build it from a string to get `1/10`.

User: how do i get exact decimal arithmetic from a float literal
Joe: Pass the string form, as in `Fraction(str(0.1))`, so the decimal you wrote is what gets parsed.

User: what causes an int and a bool to be interchangeable as keys
Joe: `bool` is an `int` subclass with value 0 or 1, so `True` and `1` hash identically and collide.

User: why does a tuple key change value when its contents mutate
Joe: The hash is derived from the items, so mutating a list inside invalidates the key's bucket and it can never be found again.

User: how do i keep a dict key stable for the life of the dict
Joe: Store only immutable or explicitly hashed values inside the key tuple, and never mutate them afterwards.

User: what makes `dict.items()` safe to iterate while modifying values
Joe: Assigning to an existing key does not resize the dict, so the view stays valid; adding or deleting keys is what breaks it.

User: how do i merge counts across several dicts
Joe: `Counter.update` or `collections.Counter` addition, which sums the values instead of overwriting them.

User: what makes a `Counter` drop zero and negative counts on cleanup
Joe: The `.elements()` view skips them, and subtracting from a missing key returns 0 rather than a negative.

User: how do i get the most common key from a `Counter` without importing anything
Joe: `max(counter, key=counter.get)` picks the key with the highest count using only the plain dict API.

User: what stops a `Counter` from counting unhashable items
Joe: Counting keys a dict, and unhashable values like lists cannot be dict keys at all.

User: how do i handle duplicates when building a list from a set
Joe: Sets have no order guarantee beyond iteration, so sort the result if you need a deterministic sequence.

User: what makes a frozenset usable where a list is not
Joe: It is hashable and immutable, so it can be a dict key or a set member and still work as a lookup table.

User: what causes `TypeError: unhashable type` at a confusing line
Joe: A dict or set lookup receives an unhashable object like a list or dict, and the failure appears at the lookup, not where the value was built.

User: how do i make a list usable as a key without converting every time
Joe: Wrap it once with `tuple(x)` at the boundary where it is used as a key.

User: why does a byte-string slice return bytes and a str slice return str
Joe: Slicing preserves the type of the container, so both keep their own element type without conversion.

User: what makes `range` objects support `len` and indexing but not slicing with a step that is invalid
Joe: `range` accepts only positive and negative integer steps of 1 in construction, so a step of zero raises at build time.

User: why does calling a function with the wrong keyword name fail loudly
Joe: Python matches keywords by parameter name, so an unknown name raises `TypeError` instead of being silently ignored.

User: why does a generator body not run until the first `next`
Joe: The call only creates the generator object; the frame starts on the first advance.

User: why does `raise ValueError("bad")` without parens not work the same
Joe: It does work, but the whole expression is treated as the instance, which is confusing when the value is a call.

User: what makes `__init__` wrong as the place to build a fresh object
Joe: Returning a different object from `__init__` is ignored; `__new__` is the hook that can substitute one.

User: what makes a `match` statement different from a chain of `if`
Joe: It destructures the subject into patterns, so one clause can bind fields and check shape at once.

User: what makes a positional argument bind to the first parameter even if the name is wrong
Joe: Positional binding is by position, so the name is irrelevant unless you pass it as a keyword.

User: what makes a generator body pause at `yield`
Joe: `yield` suspends the frame and returns the value to the caller, keeping local state alive between advances.

User: what keeps `raise Exception("msg")` from printing the message twice
Joe: The traceback prints the type first, so putting the text in the message rather than in the type avoids a doubled line.

User: in what order does `__new__` and `__init__` run
Joe: `__new__` builds the instance first, then `__init__` initializes that instance.

User: in what order does `match` try its cases
Joe: Top to bottom, and the first matching case wins, so put the specific patterns before the catch-all `_`.

User: how do i tell which parameter a positional value landed in
Joe: `inspect.signature(f).bind(*args)` binds the call and raises on a mismatch, showing the exact mapping.

User: in what order does `return` behave inside a generator
Joe: It ends iteration by raising `StopIteration` internally, so nothing after it runs and the frame cannot resume.

User: how do i raise a custom error that reads well in a traceback
Joe: Name the class for the category and pass the detail as the message, as in `raise ConfigError("missing port")`.

User: what does `__new__` have to return
Joe: An instance of the class, or of a subclass, or `__init__` is skipped and you get a `NoneType` error downstream.

User: what happens when no case matches
Joe: Nothing runs, and there is no implicit else, so add a `case _:` branch to handle it.

User: why does `f(*args)` forward a tuple cleanly
Joe: The star unpacks the sequence into positional parameters, so the callee sees them one by one.

User: what happens if you iterate an exhausted generator again
Joe: Every `next` keeps raising `StopIteration`, since the frame was discarded and cannot be rewound.

User: why is `raise` with no argument only valid inside an except block
Joe: It re-raises the currently handled exception, and outside a handler there is no active exception to re-raise.

User: why does `__init__` get called with the class as its first argument
Joe: The instance method binding passes `self` after `__new__` has produced the object.

User: why does `case _` not bind the subject to a name
Joe: A single underscore is the wildcard, which matches anything and discards it rather than assigning.

User: what happens if the tuple length and the signature disagree
Joe: You get a `TypeError` for too many or too few values, which surfaces at the call rather than inside the body.

User: how do i tell an exhausted generator from one that never ran
Joe: Keep a flag, or read `inspect.getgeneratorstate`, which reports created, running, suspended or closed.

User: how do i re-raise the same exception without losing the traceback
Joe: `raise` with no argument inside the handler re-raises the original object with its traceback intact.

User: how do i make a class that skips `__init__` entirely
Joe: Return a cached instance from `__new__`, as a singleton or an interning table does.

User: what does `case other` capture
Joe: The whole subject under the name `other`, which is handy as the final catch-all that keeps the value.

User: how do i forward keywords with `**kwargs`
Joe: `f(**kwargs)` unpacks the dict into keyword parameters, so the callee sees them by name.

User: why can a list be iterated repeatedly but a generator not
Joe: A list's `__iter__` returns a fresh iterator each time, while a generator returns itself and is one-shot.

User: what happens when you raise a new exception while handling another
Joe: Python chains them automatically, showing the original as context during handling of the new one.

User: what makes `__init_subclass__` run
Joe: It is called on the parent when a subclass is created, which is how you register or validate new subclasses.

User: how do i bind part of a subject in a `match` case
Joe: Use a sequence or class pattern, as in `case [x, y]`, which unpacks and binds both names.

User: what makes `**{}` harmless
Joe: An empty mapping unpacks to nothing, so the call behaves as if no keywords were passed.

User: why does a second pass over a generator come back empty
Joe: The frame was discarded when it finished, so re-iterating yields nothing; call the producing function again for fresh values.

User: how do i mark the original as the direct cause instead of context
Joe: `raise NewError(msg) from original`, which changes the traceback to say the above exception was the direct cause.

User: how do i pass class creation options from a subclass
Joe: Define `__init_subclass__` with keyword-only parameters and call `super().__init_subclass__(**options)`.

User: what keeps a one-element sequence pattern from matching a two-item list
Joe: Sequence patterns match exactly, so a longer list falls through to the next case.

User: why does passing a positional dict as `**` fail
Joe: Keys must be valid identifiers and valid parameter names, so a key like `"a-b"` raises `TypeError`.

User: what keeps `iter(g) is g` true for a generator
Joe: The iterator protocol returns `self` from `__iter__`, so the object is its own iterator.

User: what does `raise ... from None` do to the chain
Joe: It suppresses the context display entirely, so the traceback shows only the new exception.

User: why is a metaclass needed for behaviour affecting the class itself
Joe: It hooks class creation, so it can intercept attribute definitions on the class body, not on instances.

User: how do i match a prefix of unknown length
Joe: Add a rest element, as in `case [first, *rest]:`, which binds the tail to `rest`.

User: what stops a caller from adding an unexpected keyword
Joe: The function has to declare `**kwargs`; without it, any unknown keyword is a `TypeError`.

User: how do i write a class that behaves like an iterator
Joe: Implement `__iter__` returning `self` and `__next__` raising `StopIteration` at the end.

User: when should i suppress the chain on purpose
Joe: When the original is noise for the reader, such as wrapping a low-level parse error you have already translated.

User: what makes `type(name, bases, dict)` the real metaclass underneath
Joe: It is the default metaclass, so calling it directly builds a class the same way a class statement does.

User: what does the `|` alternative in a case do
Joe: `case 1 | 2 | 3:` matches any of those values, which is the pattern spelling of an `or`.

User: how do i keep a wrapper from swallowing a caller keyword by accident
Joe: Name the catch-all something specific and validate the keys, rather than accepting arbitrary names silently.

User: what happens if `__next__` returns instead of raising at the end
Joe: Iteration never stops, so a `for` loop spins forever unless you break out yourself.

User: why does `except Exception` not catch a `KeyboardInterrupt`
Joe: Interrupt and exit signals derive from `BaseException`, which is outside the `Exception` hierarchy.

User: how do i detect a metaclass conflict on multiple inheritance
Joe: Python raises a metaclass conflict error when two bases disagree; resolve it with an explicit metaclass on the class.

User: in what order do guards run relative to the case body
Joe: A `if` guard is checked after the pattern matches and before the body runs, so the binding already exists.

User: why does a decorator need `functools.wraps`
Joe: Without it the wrapper keeps its own name and docstring, so logs and help output describe the wrapper instead of the function.

User: why does `__next__` raise instead of returning a sentinel
Joe: It is the protocol's defined end signal, and `for` catches it and exits the loop cleanly.

User: what should a top-level handler catch to still allow Ctrl-C
Joe: Catch `Exception` and let `BaseException` propagate, or re-raise the interrupt explicitly.

User: what makes `super()` different from calling the parent by name
Joe: It follows the MRO of the actual instance's class, so cooperative multiple inheritance keeps working.

User: what keeps a guard from being a catch-all fallback
Joe: If no case matches, nothing runs at all, so a guard-only case is not a default unless it has a wildcard pattern.

User: what does `functools.wraps` actually copy across
Joe: The name, docstring, module, qualname, annotations and dict entries, which is what `help` and debuggers read.

User: what stops a `StopIteration` inside a generator body from bubbling out
Joe: PEP 479 converts it to `RuntimeError`, so a generator raising it internally looks like a bug.

User: in what order does python check multiple except clauses
Joe: Top to bottom, and the first one whose type matches the raised exception wins, so order the specific before the general.

User: why should every cooperative method call `super()`
Joe: Otherwise a later class in the MRO is skipped and the chain stops working as soon as two bases cooperate.

User: why does `match` on a subject need `__match_args__` for class patterns
Joe: Positional class patterns read the attributes named in `__match_args__` of the subject's type.

User: how do i see what a decorator did to a function
Joe: `inspect.unwrap(f)` follows the `__wrapped__` chain back to the original callable.

User: how do i end a generator early from inside another function
Joe: Call `.close()` on it, which raises `GeneratorExit` inside at the current suspension point.

User: what happens when the same exception type appears in two except clauses
Joe: The upper one always wins and the lower one becomes dead code that can never match.

User: what happens when a method forgets to call `super` in a mixin
Joe: The rest of the chain never runs, so the mixin silently becomes a dead end.

User: what keeps positional class patterns from working on a plain object
Joe: Without `__match_args__` there is no defined attribute order, so the pattern cannot bind positionally.

User: what makes `inspect.signature` on a decorated function wrong without wraps
Joe: It reports the wrapper signature, so signature-based validation checks the wrong parameters.

User: what happens to a finally block when a generator is closed
Joe: It runs, which is the hook for releasing files or locks held across the yields.

User: how do i catch one of several exception types in one handler
Joe: `except (KeyError, IndexError):`, which matches any of the listed classes and binds nothing useful.

User: in what order does the MRO put a derived class before its bases
Joe: Depth-first left to right, so `C, B, A, object` for `class C(B, A)`.

User: what does a mapping pattern require
Joe: The subject must support `__getitem__` for each key listed, and it ignores extra keys.

User: can a decorator return something other than a function
Joe: Yes, it may return any object, so a class or a partial can stand in for a function if the call sites tolerate it.

User: why is a finally in a generator a reliable cleanup point
Joe: Close, collection and exhaustion all unwind the frame, so the cleanup runs on every exit path.

User: what keeps `except A, B` from binding the exception object
Joe: The parenthesized tuple form has no target, so access the exception through `sys.exc_info` if you truly need it.

User: how do i read the MRO at runtime
Joe: `cls.__mro__` or `inspect.getmro(cls)` gives the resolution order as a tuple of classes.

User: why does a mapping pattern ignore extra keys in the subject
Joe: It only checks the keys you wrote, so a subject with more keys still matches.

User: what happens if a decorator returns a non-callable
Joe: Calling the name raises `TypeError: object is not callable`, which is a confusing failure far from the real cause.

User: how do i send a value into a paused generator
Joe: `g.send(v)` resumes and makes `v` the result of the `yield` expression that paused it.

User: how do i bind the caught exception object
Joe: `except ValueError as exc:`, which also deletes the name at the end of the block to break reference cycles.

User: what keeps `super(C, self)` from being the modern form
Joe: It hardcodes the class, so it breaks in a cooperative hierarchy where `super()` with no args follows the chain.

User: how do i express that a mapping must not have a particular key
Joe: Add a `~key` pattern or an `if` guard, since a missing key test is not part of mapping patterns.

User: how do i keep an argument count check small enough to trust
Joe: Bind the call inside the check so the wrapper validates the same way the real function would.

User: what does the first `send` have to supply
Joe: The first `send` cannot be `None`, since the frame has no `yield` to resume before it starts.

User: why does the `as exc` name disappear after the handler
Joe: Python clears it automatically so a traceback referencing the exception does not keep the frame alive.

User: why does zero-arg `super()` need the `__class__` cell
Joe: The compiler adds a closure cell for it, so the class must be created by the class statement for it to work.

User: what makes a literal string pattern match only the exact value
Joe: Patterns are equality comparisons, so no case folding or substring matching happens.

User: why does a decorator that uses `*args, **kwargs` avoid missing a parameter
Joe: It accepts everything the caller passes, so new parameters added to the wrapped function keep working unchanged.

User: why does `send` return the next yielded value and not the sent one
Joe: The sent value arrives as the yield expression's result; `send` returns whatever comes out next.

User: what stops me from using `exc` after the except block
Joe: The name is deleted when the handler exits, so relying on it afterwards raises `NameError`.

User: what happens if you copy a method using zero-arg `super` into another class
Joe: The closure cell still points at the original class, so `super()` resolves against the wrong base.

User: how do i bind the whole subject while also checking a field
Joe: Nest, as in `case Point(x=0, y=y):`, which binds `y` and matches when `x` is zero.

User: what stops a signature-validating decorator from breaking keyword-only parameters
Joe: Re-inspect the signature and honour `POSITIONAL_ONLY` and `KEYWORD_ONLY`, instead of assuming everything is positional.

User: what makes a generator a coroutine
Joe: It has a `send` method, which lets a caller push values in rather than only pull them out.

User: what is the point of the else clause on a try block
Joe: It runs only when no exception was raised, which keeps the success path out of the except body.

User: how do i keep a property working with `super` in a setter
Joe: Define both getter and setter as decorated properties and use `super().name` to reach the parent storage.

User: what keeps a guard variable from being unbound when the pattern failed
Joe: The guard only runs after a successful pattern match, so every name the guard uses is already bound.

User: how do i write a decorator that only runs once
Joe: Guard with a flag on the wrapper, or use `functools.cache` which memoizes the first call's result.

User: how do i get a value out of a two-way generator
Joe: Write `value = yield`, which suspends on send and receives the pushed value on resume.

User: why is putting the risky call in the try and the result in else better
Joe: It narrows the guarded region, so an error in the success code is not misreported as a failure of the risky call.

User: what makes a property not callable without parentheses
Joe: `obj.value` runs the getter; `obj.value()` calls the returned value, so add a method for that.

User: how do i use a class pattern to pull an attribute into a variable
Joe: `case Circle(r=radius):` binds the attribute through the positional match, given `__match_args__`.

User: what makes a decorator applied to a method receive the instance
Joe: The descriptor binds first, so the wrapper is called with `self` already in place as the first argument.

User: what happens when nobody calls `send` on a two-way generator
Joe: Each resume yields `None`, so the logic must tolerate a `None` coming back.

User: in what order do try, except, else and finally run
Joe: The `try` body runs first, a matching `except` may run next, `else` runs only on success, and `finally` always runs last.

User: how do i make a property that is also callable
Joe: Return a callable from the getter, or use a separate method, since a property has no call syntax of its own.

User: what stops a walrus inside a match pattern from being allowed
Joe: Patterns have their own binding syntax, so assignment inside them is not the walrus operator.

User: why does a plain function decorator break when used on a method
Joe: A plain wrapper is not a descriptor, so class attribute lookup returns it unbound and the instance never gets passed.

User: why should a two-way generator validate whatever it receives
Joe: A `send` can push any value, so checking the type keeps a bad push from silently becoming state.

User: what makes finally the right place for cleanup
Joe: It runs on every exit path, including a return or a raise, so a resource is released however the block leaves.

User: what keeps a property from being able to take arguments
Joe: Properties are zero-argument attribute access; use a method or a parameterized descriptor instead.

User: why is `match` faster than long elif chains for shape checks
Joe: It compiles to a sequence of tests with named locals rather than repeated attribute loads and comparisons.

User: how do i make a decorator safe on both functions and methods
Joe: Wrap it in `staticmethod` when the method needs no instance, or implement a descriptor for a real binding.

User: what does `throw` let a caller do
Joe: It raises an exception inside the generator at the suspension point, so errors surface where the data flows.

User: why should finally not raise
Joe: An exception from `finally` replaces whatever was in flight, which hides the original error and makes debugging harder.

User: in what order does the setter run when assigning through a property
Joe: The data descriptor's `__set__` runs, so `obj.x = v` calls the setter before anything else sees the value.

User: what makes `match` readable only when the cases are patterns
Joe: Used as a plain value equality dispatcher with string cases it duplicates `if` and adds syntax for nothing.

User: what stops a class-based decorator from being confused with a class decorator
Joe: One wraps methods on instances, the other transforms whole classes; the call syntax is identical, so naming matters.

User: how do i catch an exception thrown into a generator
Joe: Wrap the `yield` in try/except and the thrown error raises exactly where it was suspended.

User: how do i make a finally block safe against its own errors
Joe: Wrap the cleanup in its own try/except and log the failure rather than letting it propagate.

User: what keeps a plain attribute from bypassing a property
Joe: A non-data descriptor, one with no `__set__`, lets the instance dict win, so a stored value can shadow it.

User: should i use `match` for simple value dispatch
Joe: No; `if`/`elif` on values is clearer, while `match` earns its place on structural or class patterns.

User: in what order do stacked decorators run
Joe: Applied bottom-up and wrapped top-down, so the topmost decorator is the outermost call the caller enters.

User: why is a generator that swallows a thrown exception still marked closed
Joe: Closing after a throw ends the frame, so the generator cannot be resumed again.

User: how do i make sure a property always wins over the instance dict
Joe: Implement `__set__` as well, which makes it a data descriptor with priority over instance attributes.

User: what keeps a `match` statement from falling through several cases
Joe: Execution stops at the first match, so no fallthrough exists and later cases are unreachable for that input.

User: how do i make a decorator that can be used bare or with arguments
Joe: Detect whether the single argument is callable and return either the wrapper or a decorator factory.

User: what makes `yield from` shorter than a manual loop
Joe: `yield from other` forwards every value the sub-iterable yields, including its return value.

User: what makes `contextlib.suppress` narrower than try-except-pass
Joe: It takes exception types explicitly, so an unexpected error still propagates instead of being hidden.

User: what kind of object can intercept attribute lookup on a class
Joe: Any object implementing `__get__`, `__set__` or `__delete__`, which is exactly how properties, methods and slots work.

User: how do i group several values into one case body
Joe: Use an or-pattern with `|`, which is clearer than repeating the body in multiple cases.

User: what keeps that dual-mode decorator from treating a function as a config
Joe: Check `callable(arg)` first, since classes and callables must be told apart from option objects.

User: in what order does `yield from` delegate send and throw
Joe: Both are forwarded to the sub-generator, so it controls the flow as if it were the outer one.

User: how do i suppress exactly one exception type around a block
Joe: `with contextlib.suppress(FileNotFoundError): os.remove(path)` ignores only that one failure.

User: how do i write a reusable validating descriptor
Joe: A class with `__set_name__` to learn its name and `__get__`/`__set__` to validate on every assignment.

User: what happens when a `case` body itself raises
Joe: The exception propagates out of the whole `match`, since there is no per-case handling.

User: why is a partial applied at call time more flexible than one applied at import
Joe: Deferred `functools.partial` lets you bind values that are not known yet, at the cost of an extra object per call.

User: what does the expression value of `yield from` become
Joe: The value passed to `subgen.return()`, which is the natural way to send a result back.

User: what stops a suppress block from hiding a real bug
Joe: Name every exception you mean to allow, since an unnamed suppress is the same as a bare except.

User: what does `__set_name__` give a descriptor for free
Joe: The attribute name and owner class, so one descriptor class can serve many attributes.

User: why is `match` allowed inside a class body but not inside a comprehension
Joe: The soft keyword is statement context only, so a comprehension has no statement position to use it in.

User: what makes `functools.partial` show a confusing signature in a traceback
Joe: It reports the wrapper, so inspect `p.func` directly to see the real parameters.

User: how do i read the value a sub-generator returned
Joe: Assign the `yield from` expression; a plain `return` inside the sub-generator arrives as that value.

User: how do i build a custom exception that groups several errors
Joe: Subclass `ExceptionGroup`, or construct it directly as `ExceptionGroup("msg", [e1, e2])`.

User: why does `__set_name__` not fire for attributes set in the class dict directly
Joe: The hook runs for descriptors discovered in the class body, not for anything injected after creation.

User: how do i keep the soft keyword from breaking a variable named `match`
Joe: It stays a normal identifier except right before `case`, so rename the variable only if it sits in that position.

User: how do i print the effective signature of a partial
Joe: `inspect.signature(p.func)` for the underlying callable, since the partial object carries no signature.

User: why is a plain `return value` in a generator invisible to `for`
Joe: The for loop ignores it; only `yield from` or reading `StopIteration.value` can see it.

User: in what order does `except*` pick out matching exceptions
Joe: It selects every exception in the group that matches the type and reruns the body once with just those.

User: what keeps a classmethod from receiving the instance
Joe: It binds to the class, so `cls` is the class itself and no instance is needed to call it.

User: what makes `assert` vanish under `python -O`
Joe: Assertions are compiled out entirely in optimized mode, so never put required checks inside one.

User: why does a closure keep a variable alive after its function returned
Joe: The function object holds references to the cells it captured, so those values stay reachable as long as the closure does.

User: what happens if a generator has no `yield` at all
Joe: The call runs the body immediately and returns a value, so you get an ordinary function with no iteration.

User: what happens to non-matching exceptions under `except*`
Joe: They are re-raised as a smaller group, so nothing is silently dropped.

User: when should a method be a classmethod instead of an instance method
Joe: When it does not need instance state, such as alternate constructors or factory helpers.

User: how do i keep a required check from being optimized away
Joe: Raise explicitly, since `assert` documents an invariant rather than enforcing a contract.

User: what makes a nested function see the enclosing variable instead of a global
Joe: Name resolution walks lexical scopes outward, so the nearest enclosing binding wins over the module global.

User: how do i make a function lazily stream instead of returning a list
Joe: Add a `yield` so the call returns a generator and callers can stop before the rest is computed.

User: why is `except*` not allowed in a plain except clause
Joe: It can only appear in an `except` inside an `except*` or at a top level handling a group.

User: what makes a staticmethod different from a classmethod
Joe: A staticmethod receives nothing, so it cannot reach the class either, which suits pure helper functions.

User: what makes an `assert` message useful in production
Joe: It is the first thing a user sees when the invariant breaks, so say what was expected and what happened.

User: how do i get a copy of a captured value at closure creation time
Joe: Use a default argument, as in `def f(x=x): ...`, which binds the value when the def runs.

User: what stops a caller from forgetting to consume a generator
Joe: Nothing fails; the body simply never runs, which is a common bug in fire-and-forget calls.

User: what makes a bare `except` incompatible with exception groups
Joe: A bare handler has no type to match subgroups against, so the group would be caught whole or not at all.

User: how do i call a staticmethod on an instance
Joe: Through the class or the instance both work, since the function is just stored on the class and does not bind.

User: why is an `assert` used for type narrowing understood by checkers
Joe: A type checker treats the passing assert as proof, which removes the need for a cast in the code that follows.

User: what keeps a loop lambda from reading the final loop value
Joe: A default argument or a factory function binds each iteration's value separately.

User: how do i detect that a generator was created but never advanced
Joe: There is no public flag, so advance it once inside a test and assert on the produced value.

User: how do i retry an operation with a bounded number of attempts
Joe: Loop with a counter, wrap the call in try/except, and `continue` after a sleep so the last failure still propagates.

User: what happens if you call a staticmethod through an instance with arguments
Joe: There is no implicit `self`, so the first argument you pass is the first real parameter.

User: what stops `assert isinstance(x, str)` from breaking at runtime with `-O`
Joe: With assertions gone, the code after it runs on unverified values and fails later with a different error.

User: why does a loop lambda capture the variable rather than the value
Joe: The body holds a reference to the enclosing name, so every closure sees whatever that name holds when called.

User: why does `sum` over a generator run the body lazily
Joe: It advances the iterator one item at a time, so only one value is alive at a time and memory stays flat.

User: what stops a retry loop from retrying a permanent failure
Joe: Catch only the transient exception types, since catching everything would retry validation errors forever.

User: how do i keep a subclass overriding a factory method working
Joe: Build the result with `cls(...)` rather than a hardcoded class, so the subclass is used automatically.

User: how do i trace why a line is being reached at all
Joe: Wrap the call in a counter dict keyed by the caller, or set a trace function on the module.

User: how do i fix late binding without a default argument
Joe: Wrap the lambda in a function that takes the value as a parameter and returns the closure.

User: is a generator expression faster than a list comp then iterating
Joe: Slower per item because of the extra frame, but it avoids building the list, so the tradeoff depends on size.

User: why does a retry loop need exponential backoff
Joe: Fixed short waits hammer a service that is already struggling, so doubling the delay lets it recover.

User: what makes an alternative constructor idiomatic
Joe: A classmethod named after the shape it builds, like `from_string`, returning `cls(...)`.

User: what does `traceback.print_stack` show without arguments
Joe: The current call stack, innermost frame last, which is enough to prove you reached that point.

User: what happens if you call `del` on the cell from outside the function
Joe: The name is local to the defining function, so there is no way to clear it from another scope.

User: what keeps a generator expression from being reusable in two loops
Joe: It is an iterator, so the second loop finds it exhausted; regenerate the expression per loop.

User: how do i stop the final failed attempt from being swallowed
Joe: Re-raise after the loop when no attempt succeeded, or return a failure marker the caller must check.

User: why does defining `__eq__` without `__hash__` make a class unhashable
Joe: Defining `__eq__` sets `__hash__` to `None` implicitly, so instances can no longer be dict keys.

User: how do i capture a traceback as a string for logging
Joe: `traceback.format_exc()` inside a handler, or `format_stack()` outside one.

User: how do i reset a closure-held state from outside
Joe: Expose a reset function in the same scope, since it is the only place that can rebind the cell.

User: how do i turn a one-shot iterator into something reusable
Joe: Wrap it in `list(...)` once, or re-call the producing function each time you need the values.

User: what makes a `while True` retry loop hard to exit
Joe: There is no condition, so an uncaught exception inside is the only natural exit besides a `break`.

User: what keeps an equal-and-hashable class usable as a dict key
Joe: Define `__hash__` yourself, returning a value consistent with `__eq__` for equal objects.

User: why is `traceback.extract_tb` better than parsing printed output
Joe: It returns structured frame objects, so you can filter or serialize them without string surgery.

User: why does a nested class method not see an enclosing function's local
Joe: A class body is not a closure scope for methods, so the method resolves names in class then module then builtins.

User: what makes `itertools.chain` lazy across several iterables
Joe: It holds references to the sources and yields from whichever is still producing, one item at a time.

User: how do i write a retry helper that reports which attempt failed
Joe: Collect the exceptions in a list and include the count in the final error message.

User: why must equal objects have equal hashes
Joe: Dicts rely on it to find the bucket, so inconsistent hashes would let two equal objects live in different entries.

User: what does `traceback.format_exception_only` give you
Joe: Just the exception type and message, without any frames, which is the right length for a summary line.

User: what makes a lambda inside a class body unable to use class attributes
Joe: The class body is executed like code but lambdas inside it do not close over class-level names.

User: why does chain not need to know the lengths
Joe: It exhausts each source in turn and only advances when one stops, so it never counts ahead.

User: what does an exception traceback point at
Joe: The line where the exception was raised, then each frame outward, not the line that called the function.

User: what makes a mutable class a poor dict key
Joe: Its hash changes when the fields change, so the key can never be found again once inserted.

User: how do i find the frame of my caller
Joe: `inspect.currentframe().f_back`, though that object is unsafe to hold because frames are reused.

User: how do i pass a class attribute into a helper defined in the class body
Joe: Read it after the class exists, or make the helper a method that takes the value as an argument.

User: in what order does `chain.from_iterable` consume its input
Joe: Row by row, so each inner iterable is fully drained before the next one starts.

User: why is a traceback inside a comprehension shortened
Joe: Comprehensions run in their own frame since 3.12, so the comprehension frame may not appear in older tracebacks.

User: how do i make a mutable value object hashable
Joe: Keep a frozen identity field, or use `frozen=True` on a dataclass so it rejects mutation.

User: what makes holding a frame object risky
Joe: A traceback or frame reference keeps every local alive, so a stored frame leaks memory until it is cleared.

User: what stops a comprehension inside a class body from seeing class attributes
Joe: Comprehensions have their own scope that cannot read the enclosing class namespace.

User: what keeps chain from copying anything into memory
Joe: It is lazy, so only the current item is held at a time and the sources are read on demand.

User: how do i get the full stack including library internals
Joe: Read `traceback.format_exc()` for the formatted text, or walk `tb.tb_frame` and `tb.tb_next` yourself.

User: what makes `__eq__` accepting other classes dangerous
Joe: Returning `NotImplemented` for unknown types lets Python try the reflected operation instead of raising.

User: how do i keep a debugging frame reference from leaking
Joe: Delete it right after use, as the docs recommend, so the frame pool can reuse the slot.

User: why does a comprehension in a class body raise `NameError` for a class attribute
Joe: The comprehension's scope skips the class namespace entirely, so the name only resolves at module level.

User: how do i skip unwanted values from an iterator cheaply
Joe: `itertools.compress(data, selectors)` yields only the items where the selector is truthy.

User: what does `sys.exc_info` give inside a handler
Joe: The exception type, the instance and the traceback object, which is handy for logging without a `traceback` call.

User: why should `__eq__` return NotImplemented instead of False
Joe: It gives the other operand a chance to decide, which is what makes mixed-type comparisons behave sanely.

User: what does `inspect.signature` report for a bound method
Joe: The parameters without `self`, because binding has already happened.

User: how do i define a default from a class attribute in a method signature
Joe: You cannot reference `self` there, so pass it as an argument or set it in `__init__` instead.

User: why is islice a cheaper window than slicing a list
Joe: It advances the underlying iterator without building intermediate lists, so memory stays constant.

User: why should logging happen inside the handler
Joe: Once the block exits the exception is no longer current, so `sys.exc_info` returns `(None, None, None)`.

User: what does `__ne__` do if you define `__eq__` only
Joe: Python derives `!=` from `__eq__`, so you usually do not need to write it separately.

User: what stops a default argument from referring to another parameter
Joe: Defaults are evaluated in the enclosing scope at def time, where other parameters do not exist yet.

User: what happens if islice is asked for more items than remain
Joe: It stops early when the source ends, raising `StopIteration` with no error of its own.

User: how do i attach a traceback object to a custom exception
Joe: Accept a `tb` argument and pass it to the base constructor, which keeps the original location visible.

User: how do i define ordering on a class
Joe: Add `__lt__` and friends, or use `functools.total_ordering` to fill in the rest from one definition.

User: how do i check that a callable accepts a given set of arguments
Joe: Call `inspect.signature(f).bind(**kwargs)` and catch the `TypeError` it raises on a mismatch.

User: how do i make one parameter default to another
Joe: Use `None` and fill it in the body, since the def-time scope has no access to the sibling parameter.

User: why does islice reject a negative start value
Joe: Its arguments are counts and steps rather than indices, so a negative value has no meaning there.

User: what makes `logging.exception` capture the traceback for free
Joe: Called inside a handler it uses `sys.exc_info`, so you do not pass the traceback in yourself.

User: what does `total_ordering` not give you
Joe: It cannot supply `__eq__` or `__hash__`, so you must still define equality yourself.

User: what does `inspect.getmembers` return for a large object
Joe: Every attribute paired with its value, which can be expensive and can trigger properties, so filter with `ismethod` first.

User: what makes a keyword argument evaluated left to right in a call
Joe: Python evaluates them in source order, so an earlier `f(x=g())` runs before a later side effect.

User: what makes `itertools.groupby` require sorted input
Joe: It merges only consecutive equal keys, so unsorted data yields many one-item groups instead of real groups.

User: why does calling `logging.exception` outside a handler log `NoneType: None`
Joe: There is no active exception, so it logs a null traceback that looks like an empty error.

User: why does comparing against `None` in ordering methods matter
Joe: Returning NotImplemented for None avoids raising when sorted mixes real items with a None.

User: why is listing all members on an object risky
Joe: Accessing each value can run arbitrary property code or raise, so inspect names before touching them.

User: how do i guarantee argument evaluation order for side effects
Joe: Compute them into named variables first, then pass the variables, which makes the order explicit and testable.

User: how do i group a stream without sorting when order is irrelevant
Joe: Accumulate into a `defaultdict(list)` and append, which needs one pass and no adjacency.

User: how do i keep an error message from losing its type information
Joe: Include the exception type name in the message text when it will be read outside a traceback.

User: what makes `__str__` different from `__repr__` for users
Joe: `__str__` is the friendly display and `__repr__` is the unambiguous one for debugging and containers.

User: what makes `dir` cheaper than `getmembers` for discovery
Joe: `dir` only reads names, so it never triggers the code behind a property or descriptor.

User: what breaks if a caller mutates a list argument inside the function
Joe: The caller's list is mutated too, because the same object was passed rather than a copy.

User: what keeps a groupby key from being recomputed per group
Joe: The key function is called once per element, so a moderately expensive key is acceptable.

User: what stops a custom exception from being mistaken for a builtin one
Joe: Give it a distinct name and put it in your own module, so the traceback points back to your code.

User: what does `__repr__` need to satisfy to be safe
Joe: It should return a string that looks like a constructor call, so an object in a list is still recognizable.

User: how do i find which class defines a method
Joe: `type(obj).__mro__` and compare, or `inspect.getmodule` to identify the file that defines it.

User: how do i pass a mutable argument that the callee must not change
Joe: Pass a copy, as `f(list(x))` or `f(copy.deepcopy(x))`, or accept an immutable type instead.

User: why do i have to materialize a groupby group immediately
Joe: The inner iterator is invalidated when the outer loop advances, so consume it with `list` right away.

User: should a custom exception subclass `Exception` or `BaseException`
Joe: Subclass `Exception`, since `BaseException` would also swallow the interrupt signals.

User: how do i get a readable `__repr__` without writing one
Joe: Use `dataclasses.dataclass(repr=True)` or `reprlib`, or inherit from a mixin that formats fields.

User: what does `inspect.getsourcefile` return for a builtin
Joe: `None`, because builtins are compiled into the interpreter and have no source file.

User: why is a string argument safe from in-function mutation
Joe: Strings are immutable, so any edit inside the function produces a new object the caller never sees.

User: what does `itertools.accumulate` do with a list of numbers
Joe: It yields running totals, so `list(accumulate(xs))` gives the prefix sums of the sequence.

User: what makes a hierarchy of custom exceptions useful
Joe: Callers can catch the base class for everything or a subclass for one specific case, which keeps handlers short.

User: why does printing a container use the element `__repr__`
Joe: Lists and dicts call `repr` on their items, since the container's own display is only the brackets.

User: how do i keep a `breakpoint()` from being left in shipped code
Joe: Search for it before committing; in production it would drop the process into the debugger.

User: what keeps a generator argument from being consumed twice by the callee
Joe: Materialize it once with `list()` inside, or make the function take a fresh iterator.

User: how do i make accumulate produce running products instead
Joe: `accumulate(xs, operator.mul)` swaps in multiplication and gives cumulative products.

User: how do i keep a specific subclass from being caught by the broad handler unexpectedly
Joe: Make the subclass of the broad base only where broad handling really is correct.

User: what makes `__bool__` win over `__len__` when both exist
Joe: `__bool__` is checked first, so define only one to avoid two competing rules.

User: what does `assert` cost at runtime when it passes
Joe: One boolean evaluation, which is cheap enough for hot paths but not free.

User: how do i detect that an argument is already an iterator
Joe: Test `iter(x) is x`, which is true only for one-shot iterator objects.

User: what happens when accumulate gets a shorter initial value list
Joe: It stops when the initial values run out, which is how you add per-row offsets.

User: what happens when you `raise` a class rather than an instance
Joe: Python instantiates it for you, so `raise ValueError` and `raise ValueError()` behave the same.

User: how do i make a class support `len` without a `__len__` conflict
Joe: Define `__len__` returning an int and let the default truthiness derive from it, or override with `__bool__`.

User: what stops a debugger from being reachable in production
Joe: Nothing by default; guard entry points on an environment flag so the debugger cannot be attached accidentally.

User: what makes a sequence argument usable twice while an iterator is not
Joe: Sequences restart via `iter()`, while an iterator returns itself and is already spent on the second pass.

User: why is enumerate implemented as a C iterator rather than a plain loop
Joe: Avoiding a Python-level loop per item makes it measurably faster on long sequences.

User: why does raising an instance let you attach attributes
Joe: An instance can carry context fields, whereas raising the bare class gives no place to hang them.

User: what makes `__contains__` beat iteration for `in` checks
Joe: It is checked first, so implementing it can avoid scanning through `__iter__`.

User: how do i log the arguments a function received without a decorator
Joe: Read `inspect.currentframe().f_back.f_locals` inside a debug helper, then delete the frame reference.

User: how do i accept either a sequence or an iterator without surprises
Joe: Convert once at the top of the function with `items = list(x)` when you need multiple passes.

User: what makes `zip` return an iterator of tuples rather than a list
Joe: It is lazy, so memory stays flat and it stops as soon as the shortest input ends.

User: how do i include structured data on a raised exception
Joe: Add attributes to the instance before raising, then read them in the handler.

User: how do i make `in` work efficiently on a custom container
Joe: Implement `__contains__` against a set or dict lookup instead of letting iteration scan every item.

User: what keeps that local-reading trick out of production code
Joe: It is slow, allocates nothing useful and leaks references, so keep it in a scratch helper only.

User: what is the shortest way to count the items in an arbitrary iterable
Joe: `sum(1 for _ in it)` consumes it once, or use `len(list(it))` when you need the items again.

User: why does map with several iterables stop at the shortest
Joe: It advances in lockstep like zip, and when one raises `StopIteration` the whole thing ends.

User: what keeps those attributes from being lost when re-raised
Joe: Re-raise the same instance rather than constructing a new one.

User: what happens when a class defines `__getitem__` but no `__iter__`
Joe: The old-style iteration protocol kicks in, so `for` works by counting up from index zero until `IndexError`.

User: what does `pdb` add over `breakpoint`
Joe: Post-mortem debugging on an exception, plus non-interactive batch use through a command file.

User: why is `len` not an option for an iterator
Joe: The iterator protocol has no length, so `len` raises `TypeError` until you materialize a list.

User: how do i let map finish on the longest iterable
Joe: Pair it with `itertools.zip_longest` and a fill value, or pad the shorter input first.

User: why is instantiating an exception at module level a bad pattern
Joe: One shared instance accumulates state across raises, so two handlers can see each other's context.

User: why is relying on that protocol a bad idea today
Joe: It stops at the first `IndexError` anywhere in the body, which makes iteration subtly wrong for sparse data.

User: how do i debug code running in a thread
Joe: Call `breakpoint()` inside the thread; attaching a single global debugger will not stop the right frame.

User: how do i keep a function from silently accepting too many positional arguments
Joe: Do not add `*args`; a fixed signature makes the arity a `TypeError` at the call site.

User: what keeps filter from returning a lazy iterator
Joe: Since 3.0 filter does return an iterator, so it composes with other iterator tools instead of building a list.

User: what should a long-running process do with an unhandled exception in a thread
Joe: Catch it in the thread entry point, log it with the traceback, and decide whether the process can continue.

User: what should you implement alongside `__getitem__`
Joe: Either `__iter__` returning a proper iterator or `__len__`, so the protocol is explicit.

User: what stops `set_trace` from being visible in a traceback
Joe: It leaves no trace entry, so add a log line before it if you need to know where it fired.

User: what keeps a signature honest as the code grows
Joe: Adding `*args` to be lenient removes the error message that would have told you a caller is wrong.

User: how do i get the old list-returning filter behaviour
Joe: Wrap it: `list(filter(pred, xs))` materializes the matches up front.

User: why does an exception in a thread not stop the main thread
Joe: Threads have their own call stacks and the exception dies with the thread unless it is captured.

User: how do i make a mapping class that supports `keys` and indexing
Joe: Subclass `collections.abc.Mapping` and implement `__getitem__`, `__len__` and `__iter__`.

User: how do i make a small failing case print its input automatically
Joe: Use `assert result == expected, f"input={value} result={result}"` so the message carries the data.

User: how do i make a function reject keyword arguments it does not use
Joe: Omit `**kwargs`, since any extra keyword then raises instead of being collected and ignored.

User: is map faster than a list comprehension calling the same function
Joe: Usually comparable, and map avoids building a list, so it wins when you iterate only once.

User: how do i surface a thread failure to the main program
Joe: Store the exception and re-raise it in the main thread, or join the thread and check a result flag.

User: what do you get free from subclassing `collections.abc.Sequence`
Joe: The index, count and reversed mixins implemented on top of `__len__` and `__getitem__`.

User: what keeps an assertion from being the only test
Joe: Assertions vanish under `-O` and only cover one call path, so keep real tests that run in the suite.

User: what stops a mistyped keyword from being accepted by a `**kwargs` wrapper
Joe: Nothing at runtime; validate the keys inside the wrapper against the wrapped signature.

User: how do i apply a function lazily to an infinite iterator
Joe: map and generator expressions both stay lazy, while a list comprehension would hang forever.

User: what makes `sys.excepthook` useful for a global reporter
Joe: It is called for every uncaught exception in the main thread, which is the one place to log uniformly.

User: why should a container subclass the ABC rather than just `list`
Joe: The ABC gives the methods without the storage overhead and without promising list's exact behaviour.

User: what does `__debug__` tell you at runtime
Joe: It is `False` when optimized, so guard debugging-only work with `if __debug__:` instead of `assert`.

User: how do i log an unexpected keyword instead of dropping it
Joe: Capture the keys before delegating and record anything the real signature does not accept.

User: what makes `itertools.count` safe on an infinite source
Joe: It never ends, so it must be paired with `islice` or a `break` to produce a finite result.

User: how do i keep `sys.excepthook` from hiding the traceback
Joe: Log the formatted text then call the original hook, so the default output is preserved.

User: how do i make an abstract base class that cannot be instantiated
Joe: Subclass `abc.ABC` and decorate at least one method with `@abc.abstractmethod`.

User: how do i keep a debug-only expensive computation out of production
Joe: Put it behind `if __debug__:`, since the branch is removed entirely in optimized mode.

User: why is `f(*args, **kwargs)` in a wrapper the safest forward
Joe: It reproduces the caller's arguments exactly, so no parameter is lost to a signature mismatch.

User: how do i pull a specific number of items out of an infinite iterator
Joe: `itertools.islice(it, n)` takes the first n without materializing the rest.

User: what does `threading.excepthook` add over the global one
Joe: It receives thread arguments and the thread object, which is how you can name the failing thread in the log.

User: what happens if a subclass leaves an abstract method unimplemented
Joe: Instantiating it raises `TypeError` listing the abstract methods still missing.

User: what makes `logging.basicConfig` a no-op if called twice
Joe: Handlers are only added when the root logger has none, so a second call does nothing.

User: what makes a wrapper that hardcodes one argument subtle
Joe: A fixed leading value silently overrides what the caller passed, so bind it as a keyword instead.

User: why does `itertools.cycle` cache what it iterates
Joe: It stores the items it has seen so it can replay them, which is how a second pass is possible.

User: why is a `finally` that logs on every exit noisy
Joe: It runs on success too, so log only on the failure path or guard with an exception flag.

User: when is `abc` preferable to raising NotImplementedError
Joe: When you want the failure at instantiation time with a clear list of what is missing.

User: how do i force the logging configuration to be applied
Joe: Call `basicConfig(force=True)` to remove existing handlers and reconfigure from scratch.

User: how do i let a caller override a wrapper default
Joe: Pass the override through `**kwargs` and let the wrapper's own keyword lose to the caller's.

User: what keeps cycle from restarting the original source twice
Joe: It replays its own cache, so the source is only ever consumed once however many passes you take.

User: how do i structure a function that retries, logs and gives up
Joe: A helper that loops attempts, catches only transient types, logs each failure, and re-raises after the last.

User: why does duck typing make abc optional
Joe: Python only calls the methods that exist, so a class without the ABC still works wherever the operations are used.

User: what keeps library code from configuring logging on import
Joe: Add a `NullHandler` to the library logger and leave configuration to the application.

User: what happens when a caller passes the same argument both ways
Joe: Passing the same name positionally and as a keyword raises `TypeError: got multiple values for argument`.

User: what happens when repeat is given no count
Joe: It yields forever, so it must be paired with `islice` or it will never terminate.

User: what keeps a retry helper from retrying its own logging failure
Joe: Log with the simplest call that works and let a logging error surface instead of being caught as a transient error.

User: what does `__slots__` cost in exchange for its memory saving
Joe: Instances get no `__dict__`, so dynamic attributes, weak references and per-instance state need explicit slots.

User: why does the default logging level hide debug messages
Joe: The root logger defaults to WARNING, so INFO and DEBUG calls are dropped before formatting.

User: how do i find that duplicate-argument error before running the code
Joe: `inspect.signature(f).bind(*args, **kwargs)` raises the same `TypeError` without executing the body.

User: how do i repeat a value a fixed number of times lazily
Joe: `itertools.repeat(v, n)` yields v exactly n times and then stops.

User: when is swallowing an exception the right call
Joe: When the operation is genuinely optional and its absence is the normal path, and you record that you tried.

User: how do i keep weak references working with `__slots__`
Joe: Include `__weakref__` in the slots tuple, since the weakref slot is otherwise absent.

User: how do i see debug output without changing code globally
Joe: Attach a handler with level DEBUG to the specific logger you care about.

User: why is `functools.lru_cache` on a method dangerous
Joe: The cache key includes `self`, so it keeps every instance alive for as long as the entry exists.

User: what makes `itertools.product` materialize its pools
Joe: It precomputes the cartesian product of the input pools into tuples before yielding them.

User: how do i make a swallowed exception observable
Joe: Return a flag, log at debug level, or count it, so the failure is not invisible forever.

User: what makes a dataclass with `__slots__` generate real slots
Joe: Pass `slots=True` so the class is rebuilt with `__slots__` instead of a `__dict__`.

User: what makes a logging handler leak file descriptors
Joe: Handlers hold their stream open, so close them or use a rotating handler on shutdown.

User: what keeps an `lru_cache` on a method from leaking instances
Joe: Cache the underlying function and pass explicit arguments, so keys stay small and value-based.

User: is product on three large iterables safe memory-wise
Joe: No, it builds the pools and the resulting tuples eagerly, so use nested generators for huge inputs.

User: what stops `except: pass` from hiding a test failure
Joe: It catches everything including assertion errors, so narrow it and let anything unexpected propagate.

User: in what order does a dataclass field get its default
Joe: Fields without defaults must come before those with defaults, so ordering in the class body is significant.

User: how do i keep log formatting from being expensive on the hot path
Joe: Use lazy `%s` args rather than f-strings, since the message is only formatted if a handler accepts it.

User: how do i cache an unhashable argument
Joe: Convert it to a hashable form for the key, such as `tuple(sorted(d.items()))` for a dict.

User: how do i get the permutations of a large iterable lazily
Joe: Wrap `permutations` in a generator with `islice` so only the first few are ever built.

User: why does `contextlib.suppress` read better than a bare except for one type
Joe: The intent is inline and the allowed type is visible at the point of the code rather than in a hidden clause.

User: what makes a dataclass field default shared across instances
Joe: A mutable default is rejected for exactly this reason, so you must use `field(default_factory=...)`.

User: what stops f-string logging from working with lazy formatting
Joe: The string is built before the call, so the work happens even when the record is discarded.

User: what happens when an `lru_cache` receives an unhashable argument
Joe: It raises `TypeError: unhashable type`, since the cache needs a hash to build its key.

User: what makes combinations not preserve the input order
Joe: The output follows pool positions rather than values, so sorted-looking input is not required.

User: how do i write a context manager from a class
Joe: Define `__enter__` returning whatever the body should bind and `__exit__` accepting the three exception arguments.

User: why is `field(default_factory=list)` the right form for a list field
Joe: It calls the factory once per instance, giving each object its own independent list.

User: what makes `logging.exception` require an active handler
Joe: It pulls the traceback from the current exception context, so calling it outside one logs a null trace.

User: how do i clear an `lru_cache` without restarting the process
Joe: Call `f.cache_clear()`, which empties the cache but keeps the wrapper installed.

User: why do permutations include the original order too
Joe: It yields every ordering including the identity sequence, which makes it n factorial items in total.

User: what does the `__exit__` return value control
Joe: Returning true suppresses the exception, returning false or None lets it propagate.

User: what does `frozen=True` stop
Joe: It sets `__setattr__` to raise, making the instance immutable and therefore hashable.

User: how do i log an exception raised deep inside a library
