Joe: Catch it at your boundary, call `logging.exception` there, and re-raise if the caller must still fail.

User: what keeps a stale cache from being a correctness bug
Joe: Include every input the result depends on in the key, or expose a clear step whenever the source changes.

User: how do i ask permutations for a subset of a given size
Joe: Pass the `r` argument, remembering the output is still ordered by pool position.

User: why must `__exit__` accept exactly three arguments
Joe: The protocol passes the exception type, value and traceback, and a wrong arity raises `TypeError` on the with exit.

User: what still breaks on a frozen dataclass
Joe: Nested mutable fields are not protected, so a frozen outer object can still hand out a changeable list.

User: what keeps a library from logging at import time
Joe: Do nothing at import; logging belongs in functions, where you know the context.

User: why does a nested def inside a function run on every call
Joe: The def statement executes each time the outer function runs, building a fresh function object per call.

User: what keeps combinations_with_replacement from equalling permutations
Joe: It allows a repeated element in one slot and yields each multiset once, in a canonical order.

User: what makes a one-argument `__exit__` fail only on the error path
Joe: With no exception the call passes `(None, None, None)`, and on failure it passes three, so the arity mismatch shows up late.

User: what does `dataclasses.replace` do
Joe: It builds a new instance from the current values with some overridden, which is the safe way to update frozen data.

User: why is a module-level logger cheap to create
Joe: `logging.getLogger(__name__)` returns or creates one object and does no handler work.

User: what makes that per-call def wasteful
Joe: The code object is recompiled-free but the function object, closure cells and defaults are all rebuilt needlessly.

User: why is pairwise better than zipping with a shifted copy
Joe: It consumes the iterator once and keeps memory flat instead of materializing a second sequence.

User: how do i build a context manager from a generator function
Joe: Decorate it with `@contextlib.contextmanager` and yield exactly once from the body of the generator.

User: why is `replace` better than mutating a frozen instance
Joe: Mutation raises, so you construct the next state instead of editing in place.

User: what makes `getLogger` with the same name return the same logger
Joe: Loggers are cached by name in a registry, so the same string gives the same instance.

User: how do i build a nested function only once
Joe: Move it to module level and pass in whatever it needs, so one object serves every caller.

User: what does pairwise produce for a one-item input
Joe: An empty iterator, since there is no adjacent pair to form.

User: what does the yield inside a contextmanager function produce
Joe: The single value the `as` target binds to, so `with open(p) as f` binds whatever was yielded.

User: what does `__post_init__` give a dataclass
Joe: A hook that runs after the generated `__init__`, the right place for derived or validated fields.

User: how do i attach a handler to a child logger correctly
Joe: Add handlers to the logger itself and set `propagate=False`, or let records bubble to the root.

User: what keeps a nested def from accidentally reading a loop variable
Joe: Give it an explicit parameter instead of closing over the loop name, which removes the capture entirely.

User: how do i get sliding windows of a fixed size from an iterator
Joe: Collect into a `collections.deque(maxlen=n)` each step, which drops the oldest item for you.

User: what happens if a contextmanager generator yields twice
Joe: `RuntimeError: generator didn't stop`, because the `with` block can only bind one value.

User: how do i compute a field from other fields in a dataclass
Joe: Declare it with no default, compute it in `__post_init__`, and set it on `self`.

User: what happens when a record propagates and both loggers have handlers
Joe: The record is emitted once per handler, which is how you get duplicate lines.

User: why does a closure over a function parameter outlive the call frame
Joe: The closure stores the cell, not the frame, and the cell keeps the value alive independently of the stack.

User: what keeps a sliding window from growing memory
Joe: The `maxlen` on the deque bounds the buffer regardless of how long the input runs.

User: why is code after the yield in a contextmanager the cleanup path
Joe: Everything after the yield runs when the block exits, so it is the equivalent of `__exit__`.

User: what happens if `__post_init__` is called before a field it needs exists
Joe: Order of assignment inside `__init__` is field declaration order, so a field used earlier must exist first.

User: how do i stop duplicate log lines
Joe: Give a logger exactly one handler and let `propagate=False` stop it reaching an ancestor.

User: what makes a class defined inside a function recreated per call
Joe: Class bodies execute on every execution of the enclosing statement, so each call creates a distinct class object.

User: what does `itertools.batched` return per step
Joe: A tuple of current items, with the sentinel `None` padding the final shorter batch, from 3.12 on.

User: what happens if that cleanup raises
Joe: It propagates and may replace an exception that was already in flight, so guard it the same as a finally block.

User: what makes an inherited dataclass with defaults awkward
Joe: Base fields come first, so a non-default field in the subclass lands after defaulted base fields and raises.

User: what makes a `logging` filter cheap
Joe: It runs once per record before formatting, so keep it to attribute checks rather than parsing.

User: why does that break `isinstance` checks against an inner class
Joe: A later call produces a different class object, so instances of the new one are not instances of the old one.

User: how do i strip that sentinel from the final batch
Joe: Check the tuple for `None` and drop it, or handle the shorter last batch explicitly.

User: what makes a contextmanager generator that never yields an error
Joe: The `as` target has nothing to bind, so the with statement raises `RuntimeError` at the yield.

User: how do i resolve that ordering problem
Joe: Use keyword-only fields, which may follow defaulted ones, or give the subclass field a default.

User: how do i include a request id in every log line
Joe: Add a filter that injects the id from a context variable, so handlers pick it up via the record.

User: how do i avoid that inner-class identity problem
Joe: Define the class at module level, or compare type names instead of class identity.

User: why is a generator pipeline over a file better than readlines
Joe: It streams, so memory stays flat no matter how large the file happens to be.

User: what makes `contextlib.closing` simpler than writing a class
Joe: It wraps any object with a `.close` method, so you do not need a pair of dunder methods for a one-liner cleanup.

User: what does `dataclasses.field(compare=False)` do
Joe: It excludes the field from equality and ordering, which is right for caches and derived values.

User: what makes a context variable better than a thread local here
Joe: It propagates into async tasks automatically, so no copy step is needed at each await.

User: what stops a `lambda` from being introspected usefully
Joe: It has no name, no docstring and no source, so tracebacks point at the call line rather than the definition.

User: what makes a generator over a file keep the handle open
Joe: The file object stays referenced by the generator frame until it is closed or exhausted.

User: what stops `closing` from doing anything when close fails
Joe: It does not suppress; the close error propagates, which is usually the behaviour you want.

User: what keeps a mutable cache field out of equality
Joe: Mark it `compare=False`, so two objects with different caches still compare equal on their real state.

User: how do i clear a context variable for one request only
Joe: Use a token from `.set` and call `.reset(token)` in a finally, which restores the previous value.

User: how do i name a lambda for debugging
Joe: Assign it: `square = lambda x: x * x`, which at least gives the module a name to show in tracebacks.

User: how do i guarantee a file handle is released when iteration stops midway
Joe: Put the `with` block inside the generator so the context exit runs on close and on exhaustion alike.

User: in what order do the nested `with` items exit
Joe: In reverse order of entry, mirroring a stack, so the last opened resource is the first released.

User: how do i get a dataclass repr that omits the noisy fields
Joe: Set `repr=False` on those fields so the generated `__repr__` skips them.

User: what stops an early return from skipping that reset
Joe: A `with` scope or a middleware `finally` guarantees it, since the return path still unwinds.

User: whats the point of `operator.attrgetter` over a lambda for attribute access
Joe: It is implemented in C, so sorting a large list with it avoids building a Python frame per comparison.

User: why does breaking out of a loop over a generator not close it
Joe: A `break` drops the reference only if nothing else holds it, and collection timing decides when cleanup runs.

User: what makes an `ExitStack` better than deeply nested with blocks
Joe: It registers cleanups dynamically, so the number of resources can be decided at runtime in one block.

User: what makes `dataclass` on a class with no annotations a no-op
Joe: Fields come from annotated names, so without annotations there is nothing to generate.

User: what keeps a `contextvar` default from leaking between tasks
Joe: Each task copies the context at creation, so a value set inside one is not seen by others.

User: is a C-implemented key function measurably faster for sorting
Joe: On large lists yes, since the interpreter frame per element dominates once comparisons are cheap.

User: what keeps a half-finished generator from leaking a file handle
Joe: Use an explicit `.close()` in a `finally`, or rely on CPython refcounting as a backstop.

User: how do i add a callback to an `ExitStack`
Joe: `stack.callback(fn, *args)` registers an arbitrary callable to run on exit.

User: how do i make a dataclass keyword-only
Joe: Mark the fields with the `kw_only` marker, or pass `kw_only=True` to the decorator.

User: how do i see the actual exception in a bare `except Exception as e: log(e)`
Joe: The message is there, but no traceback; use `logging.exception` to get the frames as well.

User: what keeps a sort key from being called more than expected
Joe: Python calls the key once per element and sorts the resulting keys, so it is safe to compute something costly there.

User: how do i make a lazy pipeline that can be iterated twice
Joe: Wrap the generator function so each call returns a fresh generator rather than a stored one.

User: what happens to an `ExitStack` entered with `with` and left early
Joe: Everything registered so far is unwound in reverse, which is the correct partial-cleanup behaviour.

User: what stops a dataclass from being usable as a dict key without config
Joe: The default `eq=True` also sets `__hash__` to none, so `frozen=True` is what makes it hashable again.

User: what makes an error log useless without the traceback for a crash
Joe: The message says what failed but not where, and the traceback is what points at the line.

User: how do i sort by a property that may not exist on every item
Joe: Use a key that supplies a fallback, so missing attributes do not raise during the sort.

User: what makes a stored generator attribute a bug in a class
Joe: It is built once at init and exhausted after the first pass, so later users silently get nothing.

User: why should an `ExitStack` live inside the function that acquires the resources
Joe: Storing one on an object and entering it repeatedly unwinds everything the first time the block ends.

User: what does `slots=True` break on a subclass
Joe: Inheriting from a slotted dataclass while the subclass adds fields needs slots on both, or memory savings are partly lost.

User: why does `raise ValueError from None` hide the original context
Joe: It marks the new error as having no cause, so the chain is suppressed in the printed traceback.

User: what makes a key function that mutates the list unsafe
Joe: The sort reads the list to build keys, so changing it mid-key-calls corrupts the ordering.

User: how do i fix a stored generator attribute
Joe: Store the generator function, not the generator, and call it wherever values are needed.

User: what keeps an `ExitStack` from leaking a resource registered after unwinding
Joe: After the block exits the stack is closed, so a later `enter_context` raises rather than silently acquiring.

User: how do i keep `__dict__` on a subclass of a slotted class
Joe: Declare `__slots__ = ()` plus an explicit `__dict__` in the class body, or drop slots on the subclass.

User: what keeps a re-raise from resetting the traceback
Joe: A bare `raise` re-raises the same exception object with the frames it already collected.

User: why does `functools.reduce` need a two-argument function
Joe: It folds pairs of accumulator and item, so a unary function raises a `TypeError` on the second call.

User: why does that produce a fresh pipeline each call
Joe: Calling a generator function builds a new frame, so the values are recomputed from a clean state.

User: how do i make cleanup run even if the setup half-failed
Joe: Register each cleanup immediately after acquiring, so a failure on the next step still unwinds the earlier ones.

User: what makes `isinstance` false for a duck-typed stand-in
Joe: `isinstance` checks the class tree, so a compatible object from elsewhere fails the test even though it behaves the same.

User: how do i add a frame to a traceback without losing the original
Joe: Catch, log the original with `format_exc`, then `raise` bare so the frames accumulate.

User: how do i start a reduce from a non-zero value
Joe: Pass it as the third argument, since the second position is the sequence.

User: what stops an early break from silently truncating a shared generator
Joe: Never share one generator across callers; give each caller its own from the function.

User: what makes a lock context manager safe on an exception path
Joe: `with lock:` releases in `__exit__`, so the lock is never left held when the block raises.

User: how do i check behaviour instead of class membership
Joe: Look for the attribute, or use `hasattr`, which tests capability rather than lineage.

User: what makes an exception raised in a comprehension look like the wrong line
Joe: The frame that raised is shown, but the comprehension frame may be hidden on older versions.

User: what happens when `reduce` is given an empty sequence and no initializer
Joe: It raises `TypeError: reduce() of empty iterable with no initial value`, since there is nothing to fold.

User: what happens with a non-reentrant lock entered twice in one thread
Joe: The second `__enter__` blocks forever, since the same thread already holds it and cannot proceed.

User: what does `typing.Protocol` give over a plain duck type
Joe: A declared interface that a type checker can verify while the runtime stays duck-typed.

User: how do i make a retry helper testable without sleeping
Joe: Inject the sleep function so a test can pass a no-op and assert on the call count.

User: how do i keep reduce from failing on an empty sequence
Joe: Always pass an initializer, which becomes the result when the input is empty.

User: why does a semaphore context manager release only once
Joe: It calls release on exit exactly once, mirroring the one acquire, which keeps the count balanced.

User: what makes a `Protocol` class instantiable at runtime
Joe: It can be, though doing so is pointless; the value is in static checking and in naming the contract.

User: what keeps a hardcoded sleep from making a test suite slow
Joe: Make the delay a parameter with a small default, then override it in tests.

User: is `functools.reduce` slower than a plain loop
Joe: It is usually comparable, and slower when the function is a Python lambda because of the extra call layer.

User: what keeps a manually managed resource from being left open
Joe: Prefer a context manager, or wrap the object in `contextlib.closing` so the `with` guarantees the close.

User: why does a nominal subclass need `runtime_checkable` for isinstance
Joe: The decorator generates the check, so `isinstance` against a Protocol raises without it.

User: what makes an exception message that embeds a whole payload unreadable
Joe: Log the payload separately and keep the message to the identifying key.

User: what keeps `operator.itemgetter` from working on a dict
Joe: Item lookup uses `[]`, so a dict works, but attribute access needs `attrgetter` instead.

User: how do i add a method to a third-party class without subclassing it
Joe: Use a mixin class of your own, since Python has no extension syntax on existing classes.

User: what does `runtime_checkable` not check
Joe: Method signatures and types; it only verifies that the attributes are present.

User: how do i avoid leaking a password into a traceback
Joe: Redact before raising, and never pass the secret as the exception message text.

User: how do i index a nested dict in a sort key without a crash
Joe: Guard with `.get`, or use a tuple key whose later element is a default for the missing branch.

User: why is monkeypatching a class method globally risky
Joe: It changes behaviour for every user of that class in the process, including tests running later.

User: what keeps a raised exception from serializing a socket or lock
Joe: Attach only plain data, since the exception must survive being logged or sent over a queue.

User: what makes a nested `itemgetter` chain readable
Joe: One level per attribute, so `itemgetter("a")(itemgetter("b"))` composes into a two-level lookup.

User: what keeps a temporary patch from leaking between tests
Joe: Restore in a fixture teardown or use a context manager that undoes the change on exit.

User: why should a library define its own exception base class
Joe: Callers can catch everything from that library at once without also swallowing unrelated errors.

User: why is a lambda with a conditional return a bad sort key
Joe: It hides the ordering rule inside an expression, so the sort's intent is impossible to read off the call.

User: how do i write that undo context manager compactly
Joe: Use `contextlib.contextmanager` to save the original attribute on entry and set it back on exit.

User: what makes `except MyLibError` around third-party code too broad
Joe: It would also catch programming mistakes you never meant to ignore, so catch the specific subclasses.

User: what keeps a higher-order function honest about its callback contract
Joe: Document whether the callback may return `None` and whether it is called once per item, then test both.

User: what makes `unittest.mock.patch` restore state automatically
Joe: It records the original object and puts it back when the patch context exits or the decorator scope ends.

User: how do i detect that a callback is being called more times than expected
Joe: Count calls in the callback itself, since the contract is invisible from the caller's side.

User: how do i stop a patched attribute from leaking into another test
Joe: Always use the patcher as a context manager or decorator, so restoration is tied to a scope.

User: what makes a callback that mutates the collection mid-iteration dangerous
Joe: The iteration state is invalidated, so items can be skipped or a `RuntimeError` can appear.

User: what happens if you assign rather than call `patch`
Joe: You get a mock object with no automatic restore, so the original never comes back.

User: why is passing a generator to a function that iterates twice a bug
Joe: The generator is one-shot, so the second pass silently yields nothing instead of raising.

User: why is a MagicMock safer than a Mock for calling protocols
Joe: MagicMock configures magic methods, so `len`, `iter` and comparisons work instead of returning plain mocks.

User: how do i make a double-iteration bug fail loudly
Joe: Check `iter(x) is x` at the top of the function and convert to a list if it is an iterator.

User: what stops a mock from hiding that a method was called wrongly
Joe: Use `assert_called_once_with` to pin both the count and the exact arguments.

User: what keeps a callable default from being shared as mutable state
Joe: Defaults are built once, so use `None` plus a branch in the body for any per-call object.

User: how do i make a mock raise when a call was not expected
Joe: Configure `side_effect`, which raises the given exception when the mock is called.

User: how do i pass a callback that needs no arguments
Joe: Wrap it in a lambda that ignores its input, or use `functools.partial` with the parameters bound.

User: what makes `side_effect` an iterable act like a queue
Joe: Each call takes the next value, and when it is exhausted the mock raises `StopIteration`, which surfaces as a test failure.

User: what makes `functools.partial` a poor fit for a keyword callback
Joe: Binding keywords in a partial is allowed but easy to misread, so a small wrapper lambda is clearer.

User: why should a mock return values be specified rather than default
Joe: Default mocks return another mock, so assertions comparing to real values fail in a confusing way.

User: how do i let the caller override a partial-bound keyword
Joe: Pass the override after the partial at the call site, where it wins over the bound value.

User: how do i keep a test from depending on call order
Joe: Reset the mock in setup, or use `assert_any_call` instead of asserting a specific call index.

User: why does passing an override keyword to a partial raise instead of winning
Joe: A partial still enforces one value per parameter, so a duplicate keyword is a `TypeError` rather than a silent override.

User: what keeps a real call from happening inside a patched test
Joe: Patch the symbol at the point of use, since patching the definition elsewhere does not affect already-imported references.

User: how do i make a partial that merges defaults with caller keywords
Joe: Bind the defaults into a dict in the function body and merge the caller dict over it, rather than using partial.

User: why does patching the wrong module path produce a real call
Joe: The module that did the import holds its own reference, so patching the origin module changes nothing there.

User: what stops a partial from being pickled safely
Joe: Pickle stores the underlying callable by reference, so a partial of a local function cannot be restored.

User: how do i find which module actually performs the call
Joe: Search for the import that brings the name into the module you are testing, and patch that path.

User: how do i keep a callable pickleable
Joe: Move it to module level, since pickle stores a qualified name and cannot find a nested or lambda callable.

User: why does `pathlib.Path` read better than `os.path.join`
Joe: The methods chain, so `p / "a" / "b.txt"` replaces a join call and the path stays one object.

User: what makes a naive and aware datetime impossible to compare
Joe: One has no timezone and the other does, so python refuses rather than guessing an offset.

User: what makes `Counter` different from a plain dict for counting
Joe: It defaults missing keys to zero and returns 0 for absent ones instead of raising `KeyError`.

User: what makes `re.match` different from `re.search`
Joe: `match` anchors at the start of the string, while `search` tries every position.

User: what does `sys.argv` contain as its first element
Joe: The script path exactly as it was invoked, not necessarily an absolute path.

User: what does the GIL actually block
Joe: Only bytecode execution in one thread at a time, so pure-python loops do not parallelize on one core.

User: what keeps a `Path` from working with a string argument
Joe: Pass `Path("x")` rather than `"x"`, since the operator needs a real path object on the left.

User: how do i get an aware datetime in a fixed zone
Joe: `datetime.now(timezone.utc)` attaches the timezone so the value carries its offset.

User: why does `c["missing"]` on a plain dict raise but not on a Counter
Joe: A Counter is a dict subclass whose `__missing__` returns zero instead of raising.

User: how do i require the whole string to match
Joe: `re.fullmatch`, which anchors both ends instead of relying on a `^...$` pair.

User: why does `sys.argv[0]` differ under `python -m`
Joe: The module's file path is used instead of a script name, which changes what argument parsing must expect.

User: which parts of python release the GIL
Joe: Most C extensions do, including the socket, file and compression modules, so those calls do run in parallel.

User: how do i join a path onto a string base
Joe: Convert first, as in `Path(base) / name`, because a plain string has no division operator.

User: what does `astimezone` assume when the argument is naive
Joe: It is treated as local time, which silently shifts the instant when the machine zone is not what you meant.

User: how do i keep a Counter from inserting a key just by reading it
Joe: Use `c["missing"]`; it reads as zero without creating the entry, since `__missing__` returns rather than stores.

User: what keeps `re.fullmatch` from being identical to `match` with anchors
Joe: A trailing `$` also matches before a final newline, so `fullmatch` is stricter.

User: how do i read a positional argument from the command line
Joe: Index `sys.argv`, or better pass the list into `argparse` and read the parsed namespace.

User: what keeps a thread pool from speeding up pure-python cpu work
Joe: The GIL serializes the interpreter, so extra threads only add switching overhead.

User: what makes `/` the right join operator on paths
Joe: It reads like a filesystem path and cannot be confused with division, which is why `Path` overloads it.

User: how do i make the local zone explicit instead of implicit
Joe: Attach `datetime.now().astimezone()`, which resolves the platform zone and returns an aware value.

User: what does `most_common` return without an argument
Joe: All items sorted by descending count, so a large Counter costs a full sort.

User: in what order does `re.findall` return its matches
Joe: In string order, and it builds the whole list, so a huge input costs memory.

User: what makes `argparse` better than indexing `argv` by hand
Joe: It handles flags, types, defaults and generated help, so the parsing logic is not reinvented.

User: how should i choose between threads and processes for cpu work
Joe: Use processes, since each gets its own interpreter and its own GIL.

User: why is `os.path.join` skipping the base on an absolute second part
Joe: An absolute path replaces the whole base, since a path starting at root has no place for the prefix.

User: why does adding a `timedelta` to a naive datetime stay naive
Joe: The offset information is not part of the arithmetic, so tzinfo is left unchanged.

User: how do i get only the top three from a Counter
Joe: `c.most_common(3)`, which avoids sorting the entire mapping.

User: what makes `re.finditer` better for large subjects
Joe: It returns an iterator of match objects, so nothing is held after you move past it.

User: what does a `type=` argument to an add-argument call do
Joe: It converts and validates the string, so `type=int` gives you an int or a clean error.

User: when should i reach for threads instead of processes
Joe: When the work is waiting on I/O, where threads overlap the wait time without copying data.

User: how do i detect that a path is absolute
Joe: `p.is_absolute()`, or `os.path.isabs(s)` for a plain string.

User: what happens when you subtract two datetimes in different zones
Joe: The subtraction uses the instants and is correct, because both convert to UTC internally.

User: what keeps a tie in `most_common` from being arbitrary
Joe: Ties keep insertion order, since the sort is stable on equal counts.

User: how do i get the captured groups from a finditer match
Joe: Read `m.group(1)` or `m.groups()` on each match object as you iterate.

User: how do i make an argument optional with a default
Joe: Omit `required` or pass `nargs="?"` with a `default`, so the namespace falls back when absent.

User: what makes a process pool expensive to start
Joe: Spawning or forking a new interpreter per worker, each with its own module imports and memory.

User: what stops a `Path` from being passed to a C extension
Joe: Convert with `str(p)` or `os.fspath(p)`, since extension APIs need the string form.

User: what makes a naive timestamp comparable across machines
Joe: It cannot be; a bare `datetime.now()` means different instants in different zones.

User: how do i count words in a string with Counter
Joe: `Counter(text.split())` splits on whitespace first, then counts each token.

User: what does `findall` return when a pattern has one group
Joe: A list of that group's strings rather than whole match objects, which is easy to misread.

User: what does `nargs="+"` mean for an argument
Joe: One or more values, collected into a list, so zero of them is an error.

User: what keeps a process pool from being the answer for tiny tasks
Joe: The pickling of arguments and results plus the startup cost dominate for sub-millisecond work.

User: how do i write a `Path` out to a C function correctly
Joe: Pass `os.fspath(p)`, which is the protocol both `str` and `bytes` paths implement.

User: how do i store an instant so it can be read back anywhere
Joe: Save the aware value as UTC and convert to the target zone only for display.

User: how do i get full matches from `findall` without a group
Joe: Use `finditer` instead, since the list form loses the span information.

User: what does `nargs="*"` allow that `+` does not
Joe: Zero values, giving an empty list instead of erroring when the flag is absent.

User: how do i share state between processes cheaply
Joe: Shared memory or a manager object, since ordinary Python objects are copied per process.

User: what makes `Path` case handling platform-dependent
Joe: Comparison is by string on posix and case-folded on Windows, so never compare raw names for equality.

User: what does `utcnow` do that `now(timezone.utc)` avoids
Joe: `utcnow` returns a naive value that merely looks like UTC, so `now(timezone.utc)` is the safe call.

User: how do i count characters instead of words
Joe: `Counter(text)` takes the string directly and counts each character.

User: what makes a greedy `.*` swallow too much
Joe: It matches as many characters as possible and only then backtracks, so it stops at the last possible position.

User: how do i take a flag that takes no value
Joe: `action="store_true"` with a default of False, so its presence flips the value.

User: what makes a `multiprocessing.Queue` slower than a `threading.Queue`
Joe: Every put and get pickles the object and goes through a pipe, so there is real serialization cost.

User: why do i use `resolve` instead of `absolute` before comparing paths
Joe: `resolve` follows symlinks and returns the real path, so two different routes to one file compare equal.

User: how do i parse an ISO timestamp with `Z` at the end
Joe: Replace the `Z` with `+00:00`, or use `fromisoformat` on a string already in that form.

User: what does `Counter.update` add when given a mapping
Joe: It adds the values as counts, so counts accumulate rather than being replaced.

User: what does a non-greedy `.*?` stop at
Joe: The first position that lets the rest of the pattern match, which is usually what you want.

User: what stops a positional from being confused with an optional
Joe: Optionals start with a dash in the name, so name them that way and the parser knows.

User: what does a `threading.Lock` not protect
Joe: The GIL does not make multi-step operations atomic, so a lock is still needed around read-modify-write.

User: what keeps `resolve` from being free
Joe: It touches the filesystem to resolve each component, which matters in a tight loop over many paths.

User: what does `fromisoformat` accept in newer versions
Joe: Most ISO 8601 shapes including a trailing `Z`, though older versions only accepted output from `isoformat`.

User: in what order does `Counter` subtraction drop non-positive counts
Joe: Results keep only positive counts, so subtracting more than you added yields an empty Counter.

User: how do i capture a lazy group for later use
Joe: Wrap it in parentheses, which both groups it and turns the quantifier's extent into `m.group(1)`.

User: how do i make mutually exclusive options
Joe: Put them in the same `add_mutually_exclusive_group`, so specifying both is an error.

User: in what order does an RLock differ from a Lock
Joe: An RLock may be reacquired by the same thread, while a plain Lock deadlocks on re-entry.

User: how do i make a relative path absolute without touching the disk
Joe: `Path.cwd() / p`, since `absolute` only joins without resolving links.

User: what keeps hand-rolled date parsing out of the codebase
Joe: `datetime.fromisoformat` handles the standard forms and raises a clear error on anything else.

User: what keeps a Counter from ever holding a negative count
Joe: Subtraction discards zero and negative results, so the invariant is positive or absent.

User: what keeps a capture group from being available
Joe: Only parenthesized groups capture; bare parentheses with `?:` group without capturing.

User: what does `required=True` on a mutually exclusive group mean
Joe: The group must be satisfied, so exactly one of its options has to appear.

User: why does re-entering a plain Lock deadlock the thread
Joe: The thread waits for a lock it already holds, and nothing else can release it.

User: what makes `Path.exists` racy in a check-then-act sequence
Joe: The file can disappear between the check and the open, so catch the error instead of pre-checking.

User: what does `strftime` do with a `%` that is not a valid directive
Joe: It passes the characters through unchanged on most platforms, so a typo appears literally in the output.

User: what does `total` give you on a Counter
Joe: The sum of all counts, which is cheaper than `sum(c.values())` on some versions.

User: what does `(?:...)` buy over `(...)`
Joe: It groups for alternation without storing the text, which keeps group numbering clean.

User: how do i read from a config file instead of the command line
Joe: Parse a namespace yourself and pass its fields, or use `parser.parse_args(args=[])` after loading defaults.

User: what does a `Condition` add over a lock
Joe: It lets a thread wait for a state change and be woken, rather than polling.

User: how do i handle a missing file without a race
Joe: Open inside a `try` and catch `FileNotFoundError`, which is a single atomic operation.

User: why is `%s` in `strftime` non-portable
Joe: It is a GNU extension, so on some platforms the format string and the argument are not consumed the way you expect.

User: how do i get the most common element of a sequence in one step
Joe: `Counter(seq).most_common(1)[0][0]`, which avoids a separate sort.

User: in what order are groups numbered
Joe: By the position of their opening parenthesis, counting only capturing ones.

User: what does `parse_known_args` return
Joe: The namespace plus a list of the extras it did not recognise, which lets unknown flags pass through.

User: how do i use a Condition correctly
Joe: Always take the lock, check the predicate in a `while` loop, then `wait` inside the `with` block.

User: what keeps `is_file` from being the same as `exists`
Joe: `exists` is true for directories too, while `is_file` requires a regular file.

User: what makes locale affect `strftime` and `strptime` output
Joe: The month and day names come from the active locale, so output changes with the environment.

User: what makes a `defaultdict(list)` different from `Counter` for grouping
Joe: It creates a fresh list per missing key, while a Counter would create a zero that you cannot append to.

User: how do i name a group for readability
Joe: `(?P<name>...)`, then read it with `m["name"]` or `m.group("name")`.

User: why is `parse_args` stricter than you might expect
Joe: An unknown flag exits the program with an error, which is what makes typos visible in scripts.

User: why must a Condition wait use a while loop rather than an if
Joe: A spurious or early wakeup is possible, so the predicate must be rechecked before proceeding.

User: why does `Path.glob` return a generator
Joe: It walks lazily, so a large tree costs nothing until you start consuming results.

User: how do i keep date formatting locale-independent
Joe: Format numeric fields yourself with f-strings, or set the locale explicitly in the process.

User: how do i group rows by key with one pass
Joe: `d = defaultdict(list)` then `d[key].append(row)` inside a single loop.

User: why do named groups make `re.sub` readable
Joe: The replacement can use `\g<name>`, which says where the text came from without counting back.

User: how do i get exit code 2 for a usage error
Joe: `parser.error(...)` prints usage and exits with status 2, the argparse convention.

User: what makes `notify_all` necessary in some cases
Joe: If several waiters could satisfy the predicate, waking only one could leave the others blocked forever.

User: what makes `glob("**/*.txt")` slow on a huge tree
Joe: The recursive wildcard descends into everything, so limit depth or use `os.scandir` for targeted work.

User: what does `strptime` do on a string that does not match
Joe: It raises `ValueError` with the offending text, which is usually enough to debug the format.

User: what keeps `defaultdict` from being a data modelling smell
Joe: It hides the insertion on read, so a typo creates an empty entry instead of failing loudly.

User: what keeps a named group from colliding with another name
Joe: Names must be valid identifiers and unique in the pattern, so a duplicate is a `re.error` at compile time.

User: what does `sys.exit(0)` signal
Joe: Success, and it raises `SystemExit` which unwinds finally blocks and closes files properly.

User: why reach for a Semaphore rather than a Lock when N holders are acceptable
Joe: A Semaphore admits up to its count at once, so it bounds concurrency instead of serializing it.

User: how do i get the first match from a glob without building a list
Joe: Wrap it in `next(islice(p.glob(pattern), 1), None)` so the walk stops after one hit.

User: why is `timedelta` limited to microsecond resolution
Joe: It stores days, seconds and microseconds, so anything finer has to be tracked separately.

User: when should i use `defaultdict` over `setdefault`
Joe: When every miss gets the same default type, since defaultdict avoids repeating the lookup.

User: what happens when `re.sub` gets a replacement containing a backslash
Joe: Backslash sequences are interpreted, so escape them with a raw string or double them.

User: what keeps `sys.exit` from working inside a thread
Joe: It only ends that thread; the process continues unless the main thread exits too.

User: how do i use a Semaphore as a connection-pool limiter
Joe: Acquire before opening and release after closing, so at most N connections exist at any moment.

User: how does `glob` order the paths it yields
Joe: It walks in directory order, which is not sorted, so sort explicitly when you need deterministic output.

User: how do i convert between `timedelta` and seconds
Joe: Read `.total_seconds()` for a float, which keeps the fraction rather than truncating.

User: what keeps a `defaultdict` from being safe as a dict key source
Joe: Iterating it yields the created keys too, so a stray read silently grows the mapping.

User: how do i make a replacement insert a literal backslash
Joe: Use a raw string as the replacement, or call a function as the replacement so nothing is interpreted.

User: how do i exit the whole process from any thread
Joe: Raise the signal, or set a flag the main loop watches and exit from there.

User: what happens if you forget to release a Semaphore
Joe: The count never comes back and later acquires block forever, so release in a finally.

User: what keeps a shell wildcard from expanding in `glob`
Joe: `glob` receives the pattern as data, so a literal `*` in a filename must be escaped as in `[*]`.

User: what keeps integer division on `timedelta` from losing precision
Joe: Division returns a new `timedelta`, so the fractional part is preserved instead of being dropped.

User: how do i turn a defaultdict back into a plain dict
Joe: `dict(d)` makes a shallow copy with the default factory dropped.

User: why is a function as the `re.sub` replacement safer
Joe: It returns a plain string that is inserted literally, with no backslash escapes processed.

User: what does `os._exit` skip
Joe: It skips cleanup entirely: no `finally`, no atexit handlers, no buffer flushing.

User: what does an `Event` model well
Joe: A boolean flag that threads can wait on, useful for shutdown signals and one-shot triggers.

User: how do i glob case-insensitively
Joe: Build the pattern from lowercased parts, since `Path.glob` itself has no case flag.

User: how do i round a timedelta to whole days
Joe: Use `timedelta(days=dt.days)` to drop the time part, or divide and round explicitly for the nearest.

User: what makes `deque` faster than a list for popping from the left
Joe: A deque allocates in blocks, so an end pop is constant time instead of shifting every element.

User: what makes a function replacement slower than a template
Joe: It is a Python call per match, which adds up when there are many small matches.

User: when should `os._exit` ever be used
Joe: Rarely; a child process that must exit immediately with a specific status may use it deliberately.

User: how do i stop worker threads cleanly with an Event
Joe: Set the event, then join each thread so the loop condition ends and the worker exits.

User: what makes `rglob` duplicate matches with a leading wildcard pattern
Joe: A pattern like `**/*.txt` can match through several levels, so avoid patterns that overlap with each other.

User: what makes a naive date comparison with a timestamp string fail
Joe: One is a `datetime` and the other a `str`, so ordering raises rather than coercing.

User: in what order does `deque.rotate` move the elements
Joe: Right by default, so `d.rotate(1)` moves the last item to the front.

User: how do i reuse a compiled pattern
Joe: Call `re.compile(pattern)` once and keep the object, since the module-level functions cache only the most recent.

User: how do i run a shell command from python
Joe: `subprocess.run([...])` with an argument list, so no shell is involved and no quoting is needed.

User: what keeps a daemon thread from blocking interpreter exit
Joe: They are killed at shutdown, which means in-flight work is abandoned rather than finished.

User: why is `shutil.rmtree` dangerous without a check
Joe: It deletes a whole tree recursively and does not prompt, so verify the resolved path first.

User: how do i compare a datetime to a parsed one safely
Joe: Parse first and compare datetimes, so the comparison never depends on string format luck.

User: what does `deque(maxlen=n)` do when you append to a full deque
Joe: The oldest item is dropped from the opposite end, which makes it a fixed-size sliding buffer.

User: what stops a loop calling `re.match` with one pattern from being slow
Joe: Each call re-looks-up the cached pattern, so binding the compiled object outside the loop removes that overhead.

User: what does `shell=True` add to that call
Joe: It runs through the shell, which enables pipes and redirection but reintroduces injection risk.

User: why is a daemon thread a bad place for writing data
Joe: It can be killed mid-write at exit, so a file or database write may be left half done.

User: how do i keep `rmtree` from following a symlink out of the tree
Joe: Resolve the path and confirm it stays inside the intended root before calling it.

User: what does `zoneinfo` need that `pytz` used to require
Joe: Nothing extra; it uses the system timezone database, so the platform must have tzdata installed.

User: how do i keep a recent-items queue from growing without bound
Joe: `deque(maxlen=100)`, which evicts automatically instead of needing manual trimming.

User: in what order does alternation try its branches
Joe: Left to right, taking the first that matches, so put the more specific alternative first.

User: why is `shell=True` with interpolated input dangerous
Joe: The command string is parsed by the shell, so a value containing a semicolon runs as a second command.

User: what does `ThreadPoolExecutor` give over manual threads
Joe: A bounded pool with a queue, automatic shutdown on exit and a simple `map`/`submit` interface.

User: what makes `shutil.copy` keep the permissions
Joe: It copies permission bits with `copy2` and times with `copystat`, unlike `copyfile` which copies bytes only.

User: what happens if the system has no timezone database
Joe: `ZoneInfo` raises `ZoneInfoNotFoundError`, and the fix is installing tzdata rather than changing code.

User: what keeps a `Counter` from being the right tool for a queue
Joe: It is a counting mapping with no order beyond insertion; use `deque` or a heap for ordering.

User: what happens if two alternatives can both match the same text
Joe: The first listed wins, so order is the whole decision when the patterns overlap.

User: how do i pass a value containing spaces safely with `shell=True`
Joe: Quote it with `shlex.quote`, which escapes it for the shell rather than hoping.

User: how do i limit concurrency with a ThreadPoolExecutor
Joe: Pass `max_workers`, since the default is capped at 32 and may be higher than you want.

User: how do i move a directory across filesystems safely
Joe: `shutil.move` falls back to copy-then-delete when a rename is impossible, so it always works.

User: why is `zoneinfo` preferred over a fixed offset for real time zones
Joe: It handles daylight saving transitions, which a fixed `timedelta` offset cannot represent.

User: what does a `namedtuple` give over a plain tuple
Joe: Field access by name plus a readable repr, while keeping tuple indexing and unpacking.

User: how do i keep an alternation from swallowing a longer branch first
Joe: Reorder so the longer, more specific branch precedes the shorter one.

User: what keeps `subprocess.run` from raising on a failed command
Joe: It raises `CalledProcessError` by default only when you call `check=True`; otherwise it returns the result.

User: what makes the default max_workers a surprising limit
Joe: It is `min(32, cpu_count + 4)`, so on a big machine you get 32 threads, not hundreds.

User: what stops `os.rename` from working across filesystems
Joe: It is a single syscall on the same filesystem, so an `OSError` results when the target is on another mount.

User: what stops a stored offset from being correct across a DST boundary
Joe: The offset changes, so a datetime with a fixed offset names a different instant than the zone intended.

User: how do i add defaults to a namedtuple
Joe: Set `defaults` in `_replace` usage or use the 3.7 constructor defaults argument.

User: what makes character class `[a-z]` not match uppercase
Joe: Ranges are literal code point ranges, so add `A-Z` or use the `re.IGNORECASE` flag.

User: how do i get the exit status without check
Joe: Read `result.returncode`, which is the process exit status as an integer.

User: what does `executor.map` return
Joe: A lazy iterator of results in submission order, so a slow early task blocks later results from arriving.

User: how do i tell a `PermissionError` from a missing directory
Joe: Catch both and check `e.errno`, or use `os.path.isdir` first to distinguish them.

User: how do i get the local time zone name without a library
Joe: `datetime.now().astimezone().tzinfo`, which resolves the platform zone at that moment.

User: why has `namedtuple._make` been deprecated in newer versions
Joe: The class form accepts a sequence directly, so the helper is redundant.

User: what does a negated class `[^...]` match
Joe: Any single character not listed, so it will happily match a newline unless you exclude it.

User: what does `capture_output=True` do
Joe: It redirects stdout and stderr to pipes, so you read them from the result object.

User: why should you use `as_completed` over `map` for uneven work
Joe: It yields results as they finish, so a fast task is not held up behind a slow one.

User: what makes a write fail halfway through a small file
Joe: A full disk raises on write, and without a flush the data may only have reached the buffer.

User: what makes a monotonic clock better for measuring elapsed time
Joe: `time.monotonic` cannot jump backwards when the system clock is adjusted, so durations stay valid.

User: what keeps `_replace` on a namedtuple immutable
Joe: It builds a new instance rather than mutating, so the original stays valid.

User: how do i make a negated class stop at the newline
Joe: Add a newline to the set, as in `[^\n]` plus `re.DOTALL` is not needed since dot is separate.

User: why does capturing output with no reader deadlock
Joe: The pipe buffer fills and the child blocks writing, so use `communicate` or read before waiting.

User: how do i get the exception from a failed task in a pool
Joe: Call `future.result()`, which re-raises the exception in your thread instead of swallowing it.

User: how do i force buffered data out to disk
Joe: Call `f.flush()` for the OS buffer and `os.fsync(f.fileno())` for the physical device.

User: how do i get a high resolution timestamp for profiling
Joe: `time.perf_counter` has the highest resolution available and is monotonic, so it suits intervals.

User: what is the point of `typing.NamedTuple` over `collections.namedtuple`
Joe: It supports annotations and defaults, so the field types are part of the class.

User: why does the wildcard character skip a line break by default
Joe: The dot is defined as any character except newline, and `re.DOTALL` is the flag that changes that.

User: what does `subprocess.run` already do for you
Joe: It calls `communicate`, so captured output is read concurrently and the deadlock is avoided.

User: what happens if you never call `result` on a future
Joe: The exception is stored and never surfaces, so the failure is invisible until something else breaks.

User: when should you bother with `fsync`
Joe: Only when a crash must not lose the write, since it forces a synchronous disk round trip.

User: why does `time.time` jump when the clock is synced
Joe: It is wall-clock time, so an NTP correction or a daylight saving jump changes the value mid-run.

User: how do i make a TypedDict that a checker validates
Joe: Annotate the keys as a class, and checkers will flag missing or mistyped fields at the call site.

User: why is `re.MULTILINE` needed for anchors per line
Joe: Without it, `^` and `$` only match at the string ends; with it they also match around each newline.

User: how do i read a child's stderr only when it fails
Joe: Capture both, then inspect `result.stderr` after the call rather than streaming.

User: how do i cancel a task that has not started
Joe: `future.cancel()` succeeds only while the future is pending; once running it returns False.

User: what makes closing a file flush the buffers automatically
Joe: `close` flushes the write buffer as part of finalizing, so an explicit flush before close is usually redundant.

User: what keeps a measured duration from being negative
Joe: Use a monotonic source such as `perf_counter`, since wall-clock adjustments can make the difference negative.

User: what makes a TypedDict different from a dataclass at runtime
Joe: It is a plain dict at runtime, so there is no instantiation and no attribute access.

User: what does `re.VERBOSE` let you do
Joe: Write patterns across lines with comments and spaces, since whitespace is ignored unless escaped.

User: what keeps a long-running subprocess from blocking your loop
Joe: Use `Popen` and poll, or run the whole thing in a thread or async task.

User: what keeps a cancelled task from running in a pool
Joe: A cancelled future is never dequeued for execution, so the work function is not called.

User: why can a read file still hold a lock after close on windows
Joe: The file mapping is released only when the last handle closes, so delete-on-close semantics behave differently there.

User: how do i sleep without drifting on a long run
Joe: Sleep until an absolute deadline computed from a monotonic clock, rather than adding a fixed delay each cycle.

User: why can a TypedDict be passed straight to json.dumps
Joe: It is a dict, so the serializer sees the keys without any conversion.

User: what stops a space inside a verbose pattern from mattering
Joe: It does not, which is why a literal space must be written as `\ ` or placed in a character class.

User: how do i terminate a subprocess that overran its budget
Joe: `Popen.terminate()` sends SIGTERM, and `kill()` is the harder SIGKILL if it ignores that.

User: what does the `with` block on an executor guarantee
Joe: It shuts the pool down and waits for running tasks, so no thread outlives the block.

User: what keeps a `with open` from forgetting to close on an exception
Joe: The context manager closes on every exit path, including a raise inside the block.

User: what makes `time.sleep` the wrong tool for precise pacing
Joe: It has coarse resolution and no memory of the schedule, so cumulative error grows over many cycles.

User: what keeps a dataclass from serializing directly with json.dumps
Joe: It is a custom object with no rule, so pass `default=` to convert it to a dict.

User: how do i include a `#` in a verbose pattern
Joe: Escape it as `\#`, since an unescaped one starts a comment.

User: what happens if the child ignores SIGTERM
Joe: It keeps running, so escalate to `kill()` and then reap it with `wait` to avoid a zombie.

User: how do i make an exception handler for a whole pool of tasks
Joe: Catch around the future's `result` inside the worker, or use a wrapper that logs and returns None.

User: how do i read a file line by line without loading it all
Joe: Iterate the file object directly; `for line in f` reads lazily one line at a time.

User: how do i pause until a wall-clock deadline
Joe: Compute the remaining seconds and pass it to `sleep`, re-checking in a loop for signal interruptions.

User: how do i convert a dataclass to a dict in one call
Joe: `dataclasses.asdict(obj)`, which recurses into nested dataclasses and dicts.

User: what makes `re.escape` worth calling on user input
Joe: It backslash-escapes every metacharacter, so the text is matched literally instead of interpreted.

User: why do you need to `wait` on a Popen
Joe: To collect the exit status and release the process entry; otherwise the child becomes a zombie.

User: what makes `ThreadPoolExecutor` unsuitable for long-running tasks
Joe: The workers stay occupied, so later submissions queue behind them with no way to preempt.

User: what makes `readlines` a bad idea on a large file
Joe: It builds a list of every line, so memory scales with the file instead of staying flat.

User: what does `time.process_time` measure
Joe: CPU time consumed by the process, which excludes time spent waiting on other work.

User: what makes `asdict` slow on a deeply nested object
Joe: It copies everything, so a large tree costs time and memory proportional to its size.

User: why is building a pattern with `re.escape` safer than manual quoting
Joe: You cannot forget a metacharacter, and escaping a plain string changes nothing.

User: what does `text=True` do to the pipes
Joe: It decodes the output as text using the locale encoding instead of returning bytes.

User: how do i keep an executor from being created per request
Joe: Create it once at application start and shut it down on exit, since each pool costs threads.

User: why is iterating a file faster than calling `readline` in a loop
Joe: The iterator is implemented in C and avoids a Python call per line.

User: how do i tell cpu time from wall time in a profile
Joe: Compare `process_time` with `perf_counter`; a large gap means the code was blocked rather than computing.

User: what does `__match_args__` on a dataclass give for free
Joe: Positional `match` patterns work because the generated class lists its fields in order.

User: what makes `re.split` with a capturing group return extra pieces
Joe: Captured groups are included in the result, so an unlisted capture changes the arity of the output.

User: how do i set the encoding explicitly for a subprocess
Joe: Pass `encoding="utf-8"` and `errors="replace"` so the decoding is deterministic.

User: why is creating a pool inside a loop a leak in waiting
Joe: Each pool keeps its threads alive until collected, so loop-created pools pile up.

User: what makes the universal newlines default helpful on reading
Joe: Line endings are translated to a plain `\n`, so code written for one platform works on all of them.

User: why does `datetime.utcfromtimestamp` deprecate naive behaviour
Joe: It returns a naive value, so the replacement is `datetime.fromtimestamp(ts, timezone.utc)` which stays aware.

User: how do i keep a dataclass field out of positional matching
Joe: Mark it `kw_only=True`, which also removes it from the generated `__init__` position.

User: how do i keep `re.split` from including captures
Joe: Use a non-capturing group in the pattern, so the separators are not returned.

User: what makes `stdin` a closed pipe when not provided
Joe: The child sees an empty stdin, so any interactive prompt would read end of file immediately.

User: what does `asyncio.run` handle for you
Joe: It creates and closes the event loop, so a script needs no manual loop setup.

User: what breaks when you open a file with `newline=""`
Joe: Line endings are returned untranslated, which matters for files where the exact terminator is data.

User: how do i convert a unix timestamp to a local datetime
Joe: `datetime.fromtimestamp(ts)` uses the platform zone, so pass a zone explicitly if you need certainty.

User: what does `collections.OrderedDict` add that a plain dict lacks
Joe: Nothing important on modern versions, since dicts preserve order; it mainly helps code written for old Pythons.

User: what does `re.sub` return when there are no matches
Joe: The original string unchanged, so no exception or empty result to handle.

User: how do i feed data to a child's stdin
Joe: Pass `input=` to `run`, which writes it and closes the pipe, avoiding the manual write deadlock.

User: how do i run an async call from synchronous code
Joe: `asyncio.run(main())`, which is fine at a top-level entry point but not inside a running loop.

User: how do i preserve the exact line endings on write
Joe: Open with `newline=""` so a `\n` is written literally instead of being translated to the platform separator.

User: what makes a timestamp of zero a valid instant
Joe: It is 1970-01-01 UTC, so guard against zero only if your data uses it as a missing marker.

User: why is `move_to_end` still worth using on an OrderedDict
Joe: Reordering to the front or back in O(1) is still a real operation a plain dict cannot do.

User: how do i use a regex to validate a whole identifier
Joe: `re.fullmatch(r"[A-Za-z_]\w*", name)` anchors both ends so partial matches are rejected.

User: what does `cwd` in a subprocess call change
Joe: The working directory of the child, so relative paths in the command resolve from there.

User: what breaks if you call `asyncio.run` inside a running loop
Joe: It raises about a nested run, so use `await` instead when you are already asynchronous.

User: what keeps a write from silently mangling a binary file
Joe: Open it as `wb` and write bytes; text mode will fail on a `bytes` argument or encode it wrongly.

User: how do i get the day of the week as a name
Joe: `datetime.strftime` with a locale-dependent directive, or use the fixed English names via a table.

User: what does `defaultdict` do with the equality comparison
Joe: It compares like a dict, so a defaultdict equals a plain dict with the same items.

User: what makes `\w` include unicode letters
Joe: It is unicode-aware by default, so `re.ASCII` is the flag that restricts it to ascii.

User: why is `env=` needed to pass environment variables
Joe: The child inherits the parent environment by default, so an override must be given explicitly.

User: what does `asyncio.gather` do with a list of coroutines
Joe: Schedules them all and returns their results as a list, raising the first exception.

User: how do i read a file with an unknown encoding without crashing
Joe: Open with `errors="replace"` or `errors="surrogateescape"` so bad bytes do not raise.

User: what does `isocalendar` give over `weekday`
Joe: The ISO year, week number and weekday, which is what a lot of European reporting expects.

User: how do i detect the factory of a defaultdict
Joe: `d.default_factory`, which is `None` for a plain dict and a callable otherwise.

User: what does `\d` match in unicode mode
Joe: Any unicode decimal digit, not just 0-9, so `re.ASCII` narrows it when you need that.

User: what keeps `env=` from wiping every other variable
Joe: Build it from `os.environ.copy()` and change only the keys you care about.

User: how do i keep gather from cancelling siblings on one failure
Joe: Pass `return_exceptions=True` so each result is either a value or its exception.

User: what does `surrogateescape` preserve that `replace` loses
Joe: The original byte values are kept as lone surrogates, so a lossless round trip is possible.

User: why is the ISO week number surprising at year boundaries
Joe: A date can belong to week 1 or 52 of the previous or next ISO year, so the year field matters.

User: what keeps a `Counter` from being usable where an ordered mapping is needed
Joe: Iteration is insertion order by default, so use `most_common` or `OrderedDict` when rank matters.

User: how do i keep a currency or symbol from breaking a digit pattern
Joe: Use `\d` for the digits and put the symbol outside the class, since the class only lists digits.

User: how do i list the current environment variables
Joe: `dict(os.environ)` gives a copy of the mapping for inspection or logging.

User: what makes `asyncio.to_thread` useful
Joe: It runs a blocking call in a thread so the loop keeps serving other tasks instead of stalling.

User: how do i detect a file's encoding without guessing
Joe: Read a byte sample and check for a BOM, or use `chardet` if you must handle the general case.

User: what stops code that compares week numbers from crossing years wrong
Joe: Compare the ISO year together with the week, never the week number alone.

User: how do i count with a weight per item
Joe: Pass a mapping to `update`, since `Counter.update` adds the given values rather than counting the keys.

User: what makes a lookahead useful in a validation pattern
Joe: It asserts what follows without consuming it, so the pattern can check a boundary and keep matching.

User: what makes a missing environment variable raise `KeyError`
Joe: `os.environ["NAME"]` raises, while `os.environ.get("NAME")` returns None instead.

User: why is `run_in_executor` also a valid choice
Joe: It takes a specific executor and is the older form; `to_thread` is the shorter default-thread-pool version.

User: what makes a BOM break the first line of a text file
Joe: It appears as `\ufeff` at the start, so open with `utf-8-sig` to strip it automatically.

User: how do i get the number of days between two dates
Joe: Subtract two `date` objects; the result is a `timedelta` whose `.days` is the count.

User: what keeps a chain of `itertools.chain` from being a performance win
Joe: Each layer adds a frame per item, so two or three links are fine but a long chain slows down.

User: in what order do lookaheads run relative to the consumed text
Joe: A lookahead is checked at the current position before the next character is consumed.

User: how do i give an environment variable a fallback
Joe: `os.environ.get("HOME", "/tmp")`, which returns the default only when the variable is unset.

User: what does an async function return before it is awaited
Joe: A coroutine object that does nothing until scheduled, so calling it without awaiting is a silent no-op.

User: how do i write a file with a BOM for a specific tool
Joe: Encode with `utf-8-sig`, which prepends the byte order mark as well as the UTF-8 bytes.

User: what makes subtracting dates from datetimes noisier
Joe: The result mixes days and time, so normalize with `.days` or convert to `date` first.

User: in what order does `functools.reduce` fold a sequence
Joe: Left to right, combining the accumulator with each item in turn.

User: what does a negative lookahead `(?!)` express
Joe: That something does not follow, as in `foo(?!bar)` matching `foo` only when it is not the start of `foobar`.

User: what stops an empty string env var from counting as unset
Joe: It is set to the empty string, so `getenv` returns `""` and only a default with a default handles that.

User: what keeps a forgotten `await` from being invisible
Joe: Pass `asyncio.all_tasks` or a type checker, since the coroutine is created and discarded.

User: what makes `Path.write_text` lack newline control
Joe: It does not expose a newline argument, so open the file yourself if the terminator matters.

User: how do i add months to a date without rolling over wrongly
Joe: There is no month arithmetic on `date`, so compute the target month and clamp the day yourself.

User: how do i make reduce fold right to left instead
Joe: `functools.reduce(f, reversed(xs), init)` changes the traversal without a custom function.

User: how do i match a word boundary in a pattern
Joe: `\b` asserts the transition between a word and a non-word character, without consuming either.

User: why is `os.getenv` shorter than `os.environ.get`
Joe: It is a convenience wrapper for the common case of reading one variable as a string.

User: how do i avoid a warning about a coroutine never awaited
Joe: Always await it, or close it explicitly when you decide not to run it.

User: how do i get an atomic write without a library
Joe: Write to a temporary file in the same directory, then `os.replace` it over the target.

User: what keeps a naive month addition from raising on the 31st
Joe: Clamping the day to the last valid day of the target month is the usual convention.

User: what makes `functools.partial` an object rather than a lambda
Joe: It is a small wrapper that remembers the bound arguments, so it can be introspected and reused.

User: what makes `\b` fail on punctuation-only tokens
Joe: Punctuation is a non-word character, so no boundary exists between two punctuation marks.

User: what makes `platform.python_version` useful for bug reports
Joe: It gives the exact interpreter build, which matters when behaviour differs between patch versions.

User: what makes `asyncio.sleep(0)` different from a busy loop
Joe: It yields to the loop once, letting other ready tasks run without actually waiting for time.

User: why must the temp file be in the same directory
Joe: `os.replace` is only atomic within one filesystem, so a different directory could cross a mount point.

User: what does `calendar.monthrange` give you for clamping
Joe: The weekday of the first day and the number of days in that month, which is enough to clamp.

User: how do i see the arguments a partial has bound
Joe: `p.args` and `p.keywords`, which show exactly what will be supplied.

User: how do i keep a search from matching inside a longer word
Joe: Wrap the term in `\b`, so `re.search(r"\bcat\b", s)` does not match `concatenate`.

User: how do i get the full version with build info
Joe: `platform.python_version()` plus `sys.version`, which includes the compiler and build date.

User: when should you reach for `sleep(0)`
Joe: To cooperatively yield in a long loop so other tasks get a turn, not to wait for real time.

User: what stops a crash between write and replace from corrupting the original
Joe: The original is untouched until the atomic replace, so it either has old content or the complete new content.

User: how do i find out whether a year is a leap year
Joe: `calendar.isleap(y)`, or `y % 4 == 0 and (y % 100 != 0 or y % 400 == 0)`.

User: what keeps `partial` from being able to fill a positional argument by keyword
Joe: Positional binding must be positional, so passing that name as a keyword raises a `TypeError`.

User: what makes catastrophic backtracking possible
Joe: Nested quantifiers over overlapping patterns, which give the engine exponentially many ways to fail.

User: what does `sys.platform` tell you
Joe: The OS name as the interpreter sees it, such as `darwin` or `linux`, not the distro.

User: in what order does a cancelled task unwind
Joe: `CancelledError` is raised at the next await point, so cleanup runs in a finally before the task ends.

User: how do i clean up the temp file if the write fails
Joe: Wrap the whole sequence in try/finally and remove the temp path in the finally.

User: what makes the rule for leap years surprising in the 1900s
Joe: A year divisible by 100 is not a leap year unless also divisible by 400, so 1900 was not one.

User: what does `functools.singledispatch` choose on
Joe: The type of the first positional argument, which makes generic functions dispatch cleanly.

User: how do i spot a pattern that can backtrack catastrophically
Joe: Nested `*` or `+` groups with overlapping matches, like `(a+)+`, are the classic shape.

User: how do i detect a container or CI environment
Joe: Check for the usual markers in `os.environ`, since there is no portable flag for it.

User: what makes swallowing CancelledError wrong
Joe: It breaks the cancellation contract, so the caller waits forever for a task that refuses to stop.

User: what makes `json.dump` fail on a set
Joe: There is no JSON representation for a set, so convert it to a list first.

User: why is `zoneinfo` not thread-safe in older versions
Joe: The cache construction had a known race, so older code called `reset_tzpath` or used per-call instances.

User: how do i register an implementation for a subclass
Joe: Decorate the method with `@f.register(MySubclass)`, which adds it to the registry.

User: what keeps a regex from hanging on a crafted input
Joe: Avoid nested unbounded quantifiers, or bound the inner one, and prefer a negated class to a greedy dot.

User: what keeps a CI-specific branch from being unreachable code on a laptop
Joe: Test the environment explicitly and fail loudly rather than guessing a default.

User: how do i stop a task from the outside
Joe: `task.cancel()`, which requests cancellation rather than killing the thread outright.

User: why does `json.dumps` turn a tuple into a list
Joe: JSON has only arrays, so any Python sequence is serialized as an array.

User: what keeps a `ZoneInfo` lookup from being slow
Joe: The instances are cached by key, so repeated lookups of the same key return the same object.

User: what happens for an unregistered type with singledispatch
Joe: The base or object implementation runs, so always register at least a default.

User: why is a negated class safer than `.` in a loop pattern
Joe: `.` backtracks over everything, while `[^x]*` cannot consume the delimiter and so fails fast.

User: how do i make a script re-exec itself under a specific interpreter
Joe: `os.execv(sys.executable, [sys.executable, __file__] + sys.argv[1:])`, which replaces the process.

User: what does `asyncio.gather` do when the outer coroutine is cancelled
Joe: It cancels the children and propagates, so the work stops rather than running orphaned.

User: what happens to non-string dict keys in `json.dumps`
Joe: Int, float, bool and None keys become strings; anything else raises `TypeError`.

User: how do i handle a timestamp with no timezone in the source data
Joe: Treat it as UTC explicitly and say so in the field name, rather than guessing local time.

User: why is `functools.total_ordering` enough with one comparison
Joe: It derives the rest from the one you define plus `__eq__`, filling in the missing comparisons.

User: how do i make a regex case-insensitive only where needed
Joe: Pass `flags=re.IGNORECASE` on that call rather than compiling a global ignore-case pattern.

User: what makes `execv` dangerous mid-program
Joe: It never returns, so any code after it runs only on failure of the exec itself.

User: what keeps a shield from being the same as cancellation
Joe: `asyncio.shield` protects the inner task, so it keeps running to completion even if the awaiter is cancelled.

User: how do i keep tuple keys as JSON objects
Joe: Convert keys to strings yourself, or send a list of pairs if the key type carries meaning.

User: what makes naive timestamps from two systems unreconcilable
Joe: They may be in different zones, so a numeric difference does not mean a real elapsed duration.

User: what keeps `cached_property` from working on a slotted class
Joe: It stores the computed value in the instance dict, which a slotted class does not have.

User: what keeps a compiled pattern with flags from being reconfigured
Joe: Flags are fixed at compile time, so build a second pattern for different flag combinations.

User: how do i keep an interpreter mismatch from breaking a script
Joe: Add a version check at the top and exit with a clear message before doing anything else.

User: when should i use shield for cleanup
Joe: Around a commit or a close operation that must finish even if the surrounding task is being cancelled.

User: what makes `json.dumps` emit `NaN` by default
Joe: It allows the non-standard literals for float nan and infinity unless `allow_nan=False`.

User: how do i keep date handling testable
Joe: Inject the clock and pass the instant in, so tests set a fixed datetime rather than waiting.

User: how do i cache an expensive property value
Joe: `@functools.cached_property` computes once and stores it on the instance, unlike a plain property.

User: what makes `re.Pattern` objects safe to share across threads
Joe: Matching is stateless with respect to the pattern, so one compiled object can be reused concurrently.

User: what does `sys.executable` give you
Joe: The path to the interpreter running the code, which is what a subprocess or exec should use.

User: what makes an `asyncio.Lock` different from a threading Lock
Joe: It is bound to the loop and never blocks the thread, so waiting tasks just yield.

User: how do i get standards-compliant JSON out of a nan
Joe: Pass `allow_nan=False` so it raises, or replace the value with `None` before serializing.

User: what stops a test from passing today and failing tomorrow
Joe: A test asserting on `now()` moves with the clock, so compare against a computed window or a fixed input.

User: what makes a cached value stale after the source changes
Joe: Nothing invalidates it, so expose a way to delete the attribute and force a recompute.

User: why is a module-level compiled pattern a common idiom
Joe: It compiles once at import and is reused everywhere, which avoids the per-call cache lookup.

User: why is `sys.executable` more reliable than `python3` in a script
Joe: It matches the environment actually running the code, so no second interpreter gets picked up.

User: why must an asyncio Lock be created inside a running loop
Joe: Older versions bind the lock to the current loop at construction, so creating it early binds the wrong one.

User: what does `json.load` do with a duplicate key in the file
Joe: The last value wins and the earlier one is discarded silently.

User: why is `freezegun` useful for datetime tests
Joe: It patches the module's clock so `now()` returns a fixed instant for the duration of the test.

User: how do i keep a cached property from being computed twice in threads
Joe: The lock was removed in 3.12, so guard it yourself if concurrent first access is possible.

User: what does `match.span` give you
Joe: The start and end indices of the whole match, so you can slice the original text.

User: what makes an import from the script directory surprising
Joe: The script's directory goes on `sys.path`, so a local module shadows an installed one of the same name.

User: what does `asyncio.Event` give over `threading.Event`
Joe: The same semantics in the loop, where waiting is a cooperative yield rather than a thread block.

User: how do i detect duplicate keys while loading
Joe: Pass an `object_pairs_hook` that checks for repeats and reports them.

User: what keeps patching datetime from leaking into other tests
Joe: Use it as a context manager or decorator so the patch is undone at the end of the test.

User: what does a plain `@property` recompute on every access
Joe: Each read runs the getter, so repeated access in a loop repeats the work.

User: how do i get the text of a match rather than its groups
Joe: `m.group(0)` is the entire match, and `m.group()` with no argument returns the same.

User: how do i keep a local file from shadowing a package
Joe: Run as a module with `python -m`, or rename the file, since the script directory always comes first.

User: what makes an async sleep not block the loop
Joe: The loop advances to other ready tasks and schedules a timer callback for the wakeup.

User: what keeps `json.dump` from being pretty by default
Joe: It emits the most compact form with no whitespace; pass `indent=2` for readability.

User: how do i format a duration for humans from a timedelta
Joe: Convert with `total_seconds` and format as hours, minutes and seconds, since timedelta has no such helper.

User: why is a cached property better than manual memoization in `__init__`
Joe: It only computes when the attribute is actually read, which avoids the cost for unused values.

User: what makes `m.lastindex` useful
Joe: It names the group of the last matched alternative, which tells you which branch fired.

User: what does `sys.path` order control
Joe: Which module wins when the same name is importable from several places, first match taking it.

User: how do i run blocking code without freezing an async app
Joe: Offload it with `to_thread` or `run_in_executor`, since the loop cannot preempt a blocking call.

User: how much does `indent` slow serialization down
Joe: Considerably, since it produces a string several times larger and does more formatting work.

User: what makes `str(timedelta)` unsuitable for that formatting
Joe: It prints days and microseconds in a fixed verbose form, not a readable clock duration.

User: how do i keep a `staticmethod` from being treated as an instance method
Joe: `staticmethod` marks it non-binding, so no implicit `self` is passed when called on an instance.

User: how do i find out which alternative matched
Joe: Read `m.lastindex`, or restructure with named groups and test `m.groupdict()`.

User: how do i see what a module resolved to
Joe: `module.__file__` shows the path that was actually imported.

User: what happens if a CPU-bound function runs directly in a coroutine
Joe: The whole loop stalls for its duration, so every other task waits even though nothing is blocking I/O.

User: what makes `separators` worth setting for machine output
Joe: It controls the item and key separators, so a tight form removes trailing spaces and shrinks the payload.

User: how do i round a duration to the nearest minute
